PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

"Mosr men like to see themselves in print," but women don't. They prefer silk or satin. A WOMAN may be quite given to wringing her hands and yet not be very much of a belle. . . . .

VISITOR T "But this portrait of Mr. Bulger is a good deal inure than life size." Artist: "I know it. That is the size he thinks he is."

ETHEL : "I wonder if he loves me as he says? Re has known me only a week." Clarissa: "He may, if that's all the time he has known you." LETTv LErrovEa (coyly) "I have only seen twenty summers." Sally Sly-dig--" Is that so? Why don't you consult an oculist ? " A RUSSIAN proverb says: "When you walk, pray once; when you go to sea, pray twice; when going to be married, pray three times." IT is hard to believe that a man is telling the truth when you know you would lie were you in his place. HE: "Of course, ladies are much handsomer than men." She: "Naturally." He : "No, arti- ficially." Gus: "Why didn't you join our shooting party?" Fred: Well, I'm a poor shot, and I was afraid you might make game of me." THE REASON.—Tommy : "Why shouldn't I ever call any one a liar?" Uncle Jack: "Because, my lad, if he isn't, it will hurt his feelings ; and if he is, he will hurt yours. HE "What is that, dear?" She: "Angel food. I made it myself." He: "You'd better at it, my dear. You're the only angel in the house." STILT, PROUD: "Yes, brethren," said the con- vert, "I am an humble Christian now, but in my unregenerate days I flatter myself thatyou wouldn't meet a finer or more thoroughbred sinner in a day's walk." IN the midst of a battle the General saw a man running from a very dangerous situation. "What are you running for ? " demanded the General in a stern voice. "Sure, General," said the man, "I'm running because I can't fly." WOULD NEVER Do.—Charity Ways: "Oh, Mr. Richmoney, we are getting up an amateur minstrel performance for the Orphans' Home, and we want you to be an end man." Mr. Richmoney (in agita- tion) : "Me? End irran in a minstrel show? Why, everybody would laugh at me !" "What is the use of the sharp- pointed, spear- like spike in the top of the German soldiers' helmet, anyhow?" " What ? Don't know that? It's for use in an assault, when there's no time to load their guns aud they have no bayonets. They just put their heads down and butt."

MISTRESS (first day) : "Bridget, can you make a good mock-turtle soup?" Bridget: "Sure I can, mum." Mistress: "'Well, I'm going to market now. What shall I get you for it?" Bridget (a little flushed and perplexed) : " Jist a carrot, mum, an' a bit av a turnip an' onion, an' a very small piece av mock turtle, mum." WOMAN (to tramp): "There, I have obeyed the divine injunction to Feed the hungry,' and now I hope you will 'remember that one good turn -deserves another, and chop a little wood for me." trram : "I'm very sorry, mum, but I've got an engagement an' must be off." "Why, what have you to do?" "It is my solemn duty, mum, to go out into the highways and by-ways, an' tell hungry gents like myself that this 'ere house is a good place to git a square meal."

AN UNSUCCESSFUL TOAST.--Dr. Thomas Brown courted a lady for many years, but unsuccessfully, during which time it had been his custom to drink the lady's health before that of any other; but being observed one evening to omit this, a gentle- man reminded him of it, and said: "Come, doctor, drink the lady your toast." The doctor replied: "I have toasted her many years ; but cannot make her Brown, so I'll toast her no longer." WATER POWER.—A Boston school supervisor was passing some of the large cotton mills The river near by suggested the idea that water-power was used to run them; but to make sure, he ques- tioned the first person in sight. It chanced to be an Irishman, who was trundling a wheelbarrow of coal towards one of the engine-rooms. " Look here, my man," said the supervisor, familiarly, "do they run these mills by water ? " " Yes, sorr," answered the Irishman. " But they bile it." A SPORTSMAN'S WIT. —Over a mess table, where the parties were warmly discussing the excellence of Manton's guns, a young officer expatiated at considerable length on the superior quality of "Egg's guns," when the colonel of the regiment, a noted sportsman and wit, on being requested to give his opinion, replied that each possessed a peculiar excellence, but, in his opinion, Manton's were the best for &porting, and Egg's for poaching.

- "ADVANCE, SAPPER ! "—ln Gibraltar it is the custom for sentries to challenge all comers at night, and the person spoken to should reply, "Soldier," or " Inhabitant, ' as the case may be; the term "friend" should never be used. An infantry man was on duty in one of the principal streets, and noticing a private of the Ergineers with his sweetheart approaching, he sang out : "Halt !—who goes there?" The person spoken to, wishing to give himself as high-sounding a title as possible, replied: "Engineer and his lady." "Advance, sapper and his servant-girl," responded the sentry. "All's well." EXCESSIVE POLITENESS.—A poet gives the follow- ing lesson on politeness : Indeed, my friends, far better it would seem Were you to choose the opposite extreme— Like one down East, who an umbrella took, And from the rain gave shelter to a duck; Who to a limping dog once lent his arm, And to a sitting hen said, "Don't rise, marm" ; Nor &en to lifeless things respect did lack— said always to a chair, "Excuse my back"; "Excuse my curiosity," he said to books, And to the looking-glass, "Excuse my looks."

ADMIRABLE UNCONCERN.—A gentleman who had a weakness for conundrums and small jokes—a fact which his acquaintances greatly deplored— vies out one day with a party of friends rabbit- shooting, when a gun in the hand of the game- keeper, who was standing on the hedge, discharged itself without giving the least warning, the result being that several of the shots from it passed. through the crown of the gentleman's hat. In- stead of being much shocked by the closeness of the shave he had experienced, the wearer, with apparent unconcern, took the peforated bowler off, and, after a careful examination, held the damaged article up for inspection, with the cool comment : "That wasn't bad for a riddle, though, was it?" IMPOSSIBLE PERSONS: AND YET EVERYONE HAS MET WITH THEM.—The individual who sat down in his hat. The yokel who swallowed a story. The lady who laughed in her sleeve. The house. holder who "threw up" a window. The maiden ho melted into tears. The hasty man who tore up the street. The tailor who "cut out" his rival. The nobleman who stood on his dignity. The railway porter who was above his station. The shy little boy who could never find his tongue. The dog-stealer who caught a policeman's eye on him. The youth who split his sides with laughter. The lover who gave away his heart. " Docrolt, do you think he will recover?" The well-nigh distracted young wife wrung her hands in despair as shesspoke beseechingly to the eminent physician who had been called in a short time before,. In the room beyond lay the: prostrate form of her young husband, six feet tall, broad. shouldered, muscular, the pride of his football team in college, now lying, at six o'clock in the after- noon, on his bed, gibbering meaningless words, so weak in his half-delirium as scarely to be able to move. The sympathetic face of the great doctor assumed a graver aspect as he replied: "Madam, let us trust that with unremitting care and the utmost quiet your husband may in time assume his wonted physical tone. His long training and his iron constitution tell in his favour. 'Fre has, how. ever, been subjected to a severe mental and physi- cal strain, winch for the time being has completely shattered his nervous system. His mind at present should be kept entirely free, and particularly any allusions-to the terrible ordeal through which he has passed should be carefully avoided. At present. I am unable to state positively that he will ever be the same man that he was this morning, but with careful nursing and the use of nourishing foods, let us trust that he may be fully restored." And the good doctor, with a few parting directions, left the house, and the grief-stricken wife, as the door closed behind him, buried her fac, in her hands. "Oh, why," she muttered frantically to herself, "(lid I go shopping this afternoon, and leave him to take care of the baby?"

"CAPITAL" CHAT.

Several members of the Government who have had interviews with Li Hung Chang tell me that whilst asking many questions he carefully refrains from giving expressions to any opinion of his own. When visiting the House of Commons he asked Mr. Chamberlain and Mr. Curzon what the gold mace signified and where the members of the Govern- ment sat, but did not say what he thought of stir representative Chamber. In conversation with the Secretary for the Colonies, he sought information as to our colonial system, especially as to how their public affairs are administered, how their govenors are appointed, and upon similar points. During his interview with Mr. Goschen at Portsmouth, the Chinese Envoy not only asked many luestions about the navy, but also seeniel anxious to learn all he could about the control of our national financies, having doubtless heard that the present First Lord of the Almighty formerly held office as Chancellor ot the Exchequer.

Arthur Sewall, the American Democratic nominee for Vice-President, is a shipbuilder in Bath, iMe., where he is one of the wealthiest men in the town. He is a cold,stern man, and is regarded as very aris- tocratic by his neighbours, but he can pick up any tool in his workshop and show a lazy workman how to use it properly. Mrs. Sewall is a very intel- ligent woman, but intensely exclusive; she has the greatest horror of the publicity that has come to their private life through Mr. Sewall's nomina- tion. They have two eons, the elder of whom, Harold M. Sewall,ras appointed United States con- sul at Samoa by President Cleveland, but resigned, and leaving the Democratic Party, went to' the St. Louie Convention.

There will in all probability be a severe contest for the vacant secretaryship of the Amalgamated Society of Engiueers. The suddenness with which the vacancy has been created has caused a good deal of excitement in trade union circles, as the post is looked upon as being one of the most im- portant in the Labour world. Mr. Brereton, Mr. G. N. Barnes, Mr. W. Glennie, and Mr. George Fergu- son, of Glasgow, are already spoken of as candi- dates. Mr. Brereton is an old servant of the society at its head offices. Mr. Barnes fought Mr. Ander- son last year, and Mr. Glennie has previously been a candidate for the secretaryship. He succeeded Mr. Barnes at the head office when that gentleman i resigned his post n order to become a candidate against Mr. John Anderson. Mr. Ferguson is a delegate for the Clyde district, and in that position had the leadership of the engineers when they were locked out last year.

The reception given to Li Hung Chang on Tues- day by the London Chamber ot Commerce at Fish- mongers' Hall passed off pleasantly enough. The fine rooms were decorated with flowers, and the yellow standard of China crossed with the Union Jack was gaily prominent in the entrance hall, on the staircase, and over the seat of honour prepared for the distinguished visitor. The ex-Viceroy's progress citywards was a good deal delayed, and, contrary to custom, his hosts were kept waiting. Half an hour after the appointed time the now familiar Lonsdale chariot of canary colour hove in sight, and the guest of the afternoon entered the great hall amid great clapping of hands and pro- longed claeers, which quite drowned the Chinese hymn played by the artillery musicians. Li Hung Chang was surrounded by his secretaries, his son, and his interpreter, as he sat listening with serene impenetrability to the congratulatory speeches of Sir A. Rollit and Mr. H. Matheson.

His reply, read by Mr. Lo Fing Luh, whose pro- nuniciation of English is singularly resonant and perfect, was punctuated wit!, frequent applause, notably at the passage in which he said that for once he would depart from the usage of statesmen and make a promise. it was impossible not to notice however, that his pledge to do his best to introduce a railway system into the Celestial Empire, without any mention of special privileges to English traders,was "just a little disappointing" to a good many of the listeners, who apparently expected something very explicit indeed from the cautious Ambassador. His concluding remark that one day" London" would recall his fidelity to his promise seemed, however, to give the special point which had been previously missed, and was greeted with loud hurrahs, which the statesman acknow- ledged by rising from his chair and bowing cordi- ally ill every direction.

There. have been mysterious rumours afloat recently as to some startling development likely to create an entirely new and unprecedented difficulty in South Africa. So far as can be ascertained, this relates to a plot attributed to the Boers, at the in• stigation of Germany, to pay raid for raid, and to take the opportunity afforded by the Ifatabele trouble to'crlss the frontier, and, by seizing Mafe- king and Vryburg, aid getting control of the rail- way and our supplies, bring England to her knees, crumple up the Convention of London, and secure the complete independence of the Transvaal. Those who believe in this scheme are able to comfort the incredulous by all manner of complete details, vouched for by undeniable but always vague and anonymous authority. When asked 'why the counter-raid has not yet come off, the facile reply is that the British reinforcements sent out dis- couraged its accomplishment. The probability seems to be that the story is of the three black crows order, and is the extravagant outcome of the excited suspicions with which the Dutch and the settlers in South Africa regard every measure for self-protection.

Mr. Gladstone has almost completed the task of re-arranging his correspondence for biogralmhical purposes. He is still a most indefatigable worker, and sits at his desk for five hours every day. He has gone through the whole of the correspondence that has accumulated for years past. A vast num- ber of letters have been weeded out, but there still remain about sixty thousand missives, all of which are carefully tied up 'in bundles and docketed. In his work Mr. Gladstone has been assisted by Mrs. Drew, who is probably the only person, next to her father, thoroughly acquainted with these materials for the veteran's biography.

The visitors to the Novelty Theatre on Monday night went home little thin-king that they had been spectators of a real tragedy, and that the villian of the piece, the handsome Spaniard, had been stabbed to the heart by the Creole just before the curtain fell. There seems to be no doubt that the affair was a pure mischance. The villian had to be stabbed at the end of the piece, and unfortunately the actor played his part only too well. According to some reports it was the failure of the spring in the dagger that caused the accident. But the in- spector, who produced the weapon in court—a real dauger, not a property affair—snid there was no spring, and the blade was firmly fired in the socket. As to the rights of this, we must await furtt er in- quiry. As the poor fellow lay on the stage a brother actor, who saw that an accident had hap- pened, milled the blade out of his breast and asked him if it was all right. "Yes," said the brave man, "don't worry." The curtain had to be raised again for a few seconds, and it would seem that the dying actor remembered this, and heroically refut;ed to make a fuse. When it fell for the last time all was over.

There is the best reason for believing that since his arrival in London on leave of absence Sir Julian Pauncefote has discussed at length with Lord Salisbury the position of affairs in Cuba. Indeed, facts which have come to my knowledge warrant the statement that his Excellency was the bearer of communications, both verbal and otherwise, from the authorities in Washington affecting the future government of the island, and particularly impress- ing upon the British Government the necessity for diplomatic representation with a view to the early termination of the struggle between the Spaniards and the Cubans. It is safe to say, however, that the proposal does not meet with be approval of the Cabinet.

Some idea of the condition of affairs in Bechuana- land may be gathered from a communication received from the Rev. J. Good, of the London Mis- sionary Society at Kanye. He says that the ruder- pest has produced a fearful state of things. Upwards of 2,000 carcases of oxen lie on the veldt round his house; and'about 30,000 bead of cattle belonging to the tribe to which he ministers have died.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY AUGUST 15. 1896

Mainly owing to the influence of the late Sir John Cowell, Master of the Household, the installation of the electric light was brought to Osborne, in 1893, and no- where is it seen to better advantage than in the magni- ficent Durbar Room where the illumination enhances the delicate Indian decoration. The engine used for the system is about twent-F-two horse power, and is housed in a building at the east entrance of the Palace. Along- side ef the engine house, is the accumulator room, filled with glass cella,which are charged during the day, and diatributed at night. The Queen, however,fer her own •personal use objects to the electric light,and still prefers candles and oil lamps. Possible burglars seem to be the one great terror of Madame Patti's life, at Craig-y-nos Castle, her beautiful South Wales residence. All the window shutters are fitted with electrie bells, which start ringing at the slightest touch, while by the same machinery a gun is fired and a number of fierce dogs are let loose in the grounds. Up till now this unique system of warning has never been called into play, the nearest approach to a night alarm being on the occasion of the visit of a well known journalist, who feeling in need of more air threw open his windon at 2 a.m. There was then a demon- stration worthy of a comic opera. Apropos of burglars, those of us who indulge in sea bathing will do well to dispense with valuables when setting forth in search of a machine or tent, or our morn- ing dip. Only last week a friend of mine, on returning to her own particular tent found that her valuable watch and chain, a pocket-book containing bank notes to the value of ten pounds and two handsome rings, had been stolen. The inconsiderate thief had also removed my friend's garments with the exception of her shoes and hat, and she had to proceed to her hotel clad in a bath- ing dress and a cloak extemporised out of bath towels. The light fingered fraternity, who regard the holiday season as their own particular harvest time, are not slow to take advantage anywhere of the general faith of humanity in the honestY of its fellow creatures, and there is very little doubt that it is far wiser to leave all valuables at home or in safe keeping, when starting for a seaside or country holiday. Although in private lite Americans are not in the habit of indulging in hand-shaking more than other nations do, the practice of shaking hands with states- men, public characters, and their wives, amounts to a positive mania. Mrs. Cleveland, the wife of the Ameri- can President, can now shake hands with 5,000 people in two hours and feel the freshest woman in the company when all is done, hut her sufferings at her first recep- tions at the White House, as grar hically described by the pens of a thousand sympathetic journalists, were enough to melt the heart of the most uncompromising foe of Republican institutions. Mrs. Cleveland's secret is, that she grasps the outstretched hand and gives the initiative hand-shake. The plan of shaking each hand herself, and never allowing anyone to shake her hand results in far less fatigue than if she reversed the order. The two ladies who received with her at the last gather- ing in the White House were not coached in this respect, and it was noticeable that great fatigue was experienced by them from the strain of the two hours' hand-shaking. It is really somev hat strange that no ineenious person has thought of obviating this difficulty by mechanical means. A hand-shaking machine, behind which the statesman and his wife could stand and look happy, while their admirers filed past them, and exerted their strength on the automatic hand shaker, would appar- ently be a much appreciated invention among ourH American cousins. I Few of us, as we take our walks abroad, can fail to notice the increase in the number of women with auburn or Titian-hued hair, and the decrease in the number of those who wear golden locks. The craze for peroxide of hydrogen is more than on the wane, but the demand for henna is so great that while none, or next to none, was imported a couple of years ago, thousands of pounds worth is now brought annually to this country. Indeed, so generally have chorus girls gone in for red hair, that one of our theatrical managers told me the other day, that nearly every woman who has of late, applied to him far an engagement, boasts of heir of the " sunset tint." By the way, those of my readers who are not already acquainted with the feet, 'nay be interested to know that many of the most lovely women of classical and modern renown, were women with hair of some shade of red—Helen of Troy, Cleopatra, Marie Stewart, Queen Elizabeth, Annie of Austria and others. Golf capes this season show no new departure, except that the shoulder dart is rather more frequently in evi- dence. Despite the growing demand for plain self colours, there is the most fascinating selection of n. utral browns and greys; while for seAside and country wear red brown reversible tweed, plaided on the inside, is most serviceable and eftective. Collars this autumn will not be worn so high as they have been,they are to roll over and not stand straight up to the ears unless they are of the m isculine coat pattern Strappings, braid, and buttons, are the recognised trim- ming of all simple cloth coats and capes, with a little velvet facing for the collar. Fancy huttonr will not be used for cloth garments, they will be either of bone or pearl, very large or quite small. Leather bound skirts are dying out,partly on account of the difficulty in procuring the leather ready cut into strips and the trouble of mounting, and party owing to the fact that unless boots of a particularly strong and durable kind are worn with these skirts,the two leathers are apt to disagree,and display signs of the friction which is inevitable. Experience has taught me that with a gown of suitable length,viz., three or four inches off the ground,a good braid or velvet binding answers all the purpose, and is much more easily renewed. The newest felt hats for eutumn wear possess high crowns of sugar loaf shape. They are decidedly becom- ing, exrept perhaps to tall girls ; who by the way are more abundant than ever. A recent critic complained tbhaahti yl i tt thl ies hwandmhearirpheanded.. bg eo criaeunque t to ain io gfircetatnexatne dn tptrhoe- little woman has "gone out "in real life. So much the

better, for though in stories there is something fascinat- ing in tlas heroine's bird-like fluttering', in her nestling timidly lb the hero's protecting arms.the charming fa ry was apt to become an invalid in mature life and spend half her time upon the sofa. The woman who reclines cn a sofa has also vanished from fiction, a least as an in- teresting and attractive character, and to die of con- sumption is no longer romantic. Health is in fashion. VEGETABLE MARROW PRESERVS is delicious—Peel some good sound marrows,carefulty remove all seed and pulp, cut them up into neat dice about one inch square, weigh them, and allow one pound of sugar to each pound of fruit. Place the sugar and fruit in a.basin, and leave it all night, Next day turn into a presetving pan, and boil till the pieces are transparent. Flavor either with ginger cut in small pieces, or with some lemon juice and a little of the thinly-pared rind cut into tiny strips. Great care must be taken not to mash up the dice when stirring. To ascertain when it is cooked enough, try a little on a plate, if the syrup sets then it is ready—Be careful that it does not boil fast or the syrup will cry- stallise.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE.

I should like to tell you of a lovely dress which I saw a day or two ago. It was of white Louis Seize silk, strewn with wreaths of tiny flowers in delicate tints, caught with little slender bows of palest blue. The skirt had a waved line of white quilled chiffon about a quarter of a yard above the hem, caught up with bows of palest blue velvet baby ribbon, wired and twisted to correspond with its pretty little prototypes in the design. The coat-shaped bodice had a basque almost devoid of fulness, and cut away somewhat in front. The vest was of fold after fold of pale blue chiffon, embroidered all over with Louis Seize bows in silver, and a sash of pale blue miroir velvet was fastened lightly to one side of the waist with a bow in fine old paste. The coat was simply rolled back very little, and showed a white satin lining edged with silver cord.

In regard to headgear fashion is ever changing. Perhaps you would like a hint as to a cycling hat. One of the neatest I have seen was of natural straw. with the crown raised at the back. It had a black velvet band and bow at the right side, with a black chiffon bow and black osprey at the left. For ordinary wear a beautiful toque is of pale sea-green rush straw, with a sunk crown of ivory satin over- laid with horsehair guipure; the trimming is a couple of black clliffon rosettes on either side of the front peak, crush roses shading from white through green to lemon yellow, which rest on the hair at the back, and a bunch of black tips with a curling white osprey at one side. As a fine-weather alternative to the toque is a picture hat of butter- cup straw, trimmed with a draping of old lace, a wreath of pink roses, an upstanding ruche of black chiffon spotted with white, and high bows of white ribbon with graduated pink satin stripe. Poppey- coloured hats are much in vogue, heavily trimmed, and all bearing the new bunched quills.

Of blouses there are some really lovely f10we1.,.; muslin specimens to be had just now. One ti,at particularly struck my taste was a creamy muslin with pink rosebuds ani green foliage; it was decorated with broad butter-coloured Valenciennes lace. I must tell you, too, of an exquisitely fitting blouse bodice of ivory glace silk with pale maize and narrow black stripe, flowered with pale blue. This was slightly full in the back, but drawn closely down with a simulated Swiss belt, edged with inch-wide maize ribbon. The front had short Eton fronts, and a double vest, one of these pouched and of the silk, the inner one of chiffon and real lace. This was worn over a skirt of ivory alpaca mounted on maize silk, with a narrow appliqm-S border of pale blue cloth embroidered in black and ecru. - - _ - -

----- ..._. For those who wear black or dark colours, the short zouave of guipure is a particularly useful and dainty addition, and more especially so when clever fingers bring out the leading scrolls with a tracery of bead and add jewels or sequins to the central patterns.

You mentiee a chi! rs dea.s, and I quite appre- ciate the desci 9 u i VG' inc. One I saw the other day was a white sertie frock ornamented with white braid. The deep white linen collar is edged with lace, and the sash is white, or whatever soft bright colour may best suit the little wearer. Considering the changeable nature of the weather recently I am told to remark to you that a good waterproof should be a cherished possession with every Englishwoman," and more especially when she is going on her travels, for in our delightful climate it is never safe to leave home even for one week without being prepared for all sorts of weather. There is no need for a waterproof to be the ugly, unbecoming garment one so often.sees, nor for it to be made of malodorous mackintosh, for by the famous Cravenette process, any cloth is rendered completely waterproof without injury to the texture, and without imparting the least suspicion of a disagreeable odour, for rubber does not enter into the composition of the proofing fluid. Believe me, as ever, Your loving JENNISI

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Duff House,Banffshire,where the Duke and Duchess of Fife are spending a few weeks before proceeding to the Upper Deeside for the shooting season, is a stately house which was built early in the reign of George 111., in imitation of the Villa Borghese. It is within a short dis- tance of the sea, and has fine gardens and an exten- sive and richly wooded demense,which is intersected by the river Deveron, and contains one of the best collec- tions of pictures in the North of Scotland, besides a valu- able library.

Speakin4 of Scot.land reminds me of the bagpipes, and a young woman who resides in California. It appears she is only eighteen years of age, that she is of Scottish stock, and is a slightly built maiden, but " she carries herself with the proverbial stateliness of the piper, and marches to the tune she plays." Moreover she has be- come such an adept of the art that she is " sought all along the coast, to give colour to the gatherings of the men from Burns' land." It is not often we hesr of mem- bers of our sex devoting themselves to a practical study of the bagpipe, but pos ibly this new idea will open out a field of employment for Scotch lassics who find the problem of bow to earn their own living, a difficult one to solve.

GRIM STORY OF A HOSPITAL FAILURE.

A representative of the "South Bucks Standard "re- cently visited Paul's-row, High Wycombe, to investigate an alleged extraordinary escape from death. Mr. and Mrs. Gray, one of whose daughters was the, subject of the rumour, gave authentic information. "What was the cause of your daughter's danger,Mrs.

Gray ? " " For quite eight years," replied the muther, " she has been a sufferer frnm swellings in the legs and feet, fearful attacks of palpitation at the heart, and weari- ness—dreadful weariness. We have had her laid up for three months at a time, and have taken her to St. George's Hospital in London. When we got there—poor girl—they told us it was useless to go into any hospital. They said she might as well stay at home, for they could do her no good. Other doctors attended her, but she found no relief. You could not see the shape of her ankles nor her toes at all, her feet were so swollen. After great suffering, we bought her somelof Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People."

"How did you come to hear of these ?" "It was my husband who first beard of them. He read in the paper about a case something similar that had been miraculously cured by them."

" How long ago was it when you first had the. Pills ?"

"Just before Wycombe Fair last year," replied Mrs. Gray. "She has gone six weeks and could not get on her feet at all. The neighbours all said it was impossible for her to live, she was dropsical. The doctors said there was no cure. She has never had a day in bed ever since we obtained Dr. Williams' Pills. They have done what no doctor could do. I could not explain to you, sir, hew thankful we are for the Pills. She bad to be fed like 'a bad.'

Miss Gray is now in splendid health, thanks to the tonic virtue of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which cure not only cases like the above, but locomotor ataxy, sciatica, palpitations,low vitalitv,consumption, and the disorders which arise from impoverished blood, such as antemia, pale and sallow complexion, weakness, loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the baA,nervous headache, early decay, all forms of female weakness, and wasting strength from any cause. These Pills are sold by chemists and by Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn viaduct, London, EC., at 2s. 9d. a box, or six for 13s. 9d. Genuine only with the full name. The marriage of Capitols and Herbert, and that of Clara and of Traverse, was fixed to take place upon the first of August, which was the twenty- first birthday of the doctor's daughter, and also the twenty-fifth anniversary of the wedding of Ira Warfleld and Marsh Rocks. German husbands and wives have a beautiful custom of keeping the twenty-fifth anniversary of their marriage by a festival which they call the " Silver Wedding." And thus Major Warfield and Marsh resolved to keep this first of August, and farther to honour the occasion by uniting the hands of their younz people. There was but one cloud upon the happiness of "Capitola ; this was the approaching execution of Black Donald. No one else seemed to care about the matter, untit a circumstance occurred which painfully aroused their interest.

This was the fact that the Governor, through the solicitation of certain ministers of the gospel, who represented the condemned as utterly unprepared to meet his fate, had respited him until the first of August, at which time he wished the prisoner to be made to understand that his sentence would cer- tainly, without farther delay, be carried into effect. This carried a sort of consternation into the heart of every member of the ITurricane Hall household The idea of Black Donald being hung in their Immediate neighbourhood upon their wedding day was appalling! Yet there was no help for it, unless their wedding was postponed to another occasion than that upon which 01(1 Hurricane had set his heart. No one knew what to do.

Cap fretted herself almost sick. She bad cudgelled her brains to no purpose. She had not been at t to think of any plan by which she c,ouli deliver Blick Donald. That night the remnant of Black Donald's band were assembled in their first old haunt, the Old Road Inn. They had met for a two-fold purpose— to bury their old matron, Mother Raven, who, since the death of her patron and the apprehension of her Captain, had returned to the inn to die—and to bewail the fate of their leader, whose execution was expected to come off the next day. The men laid the poor old woman in her wood- land grave, and assembled in the kitchen to keep a death watch in sympathy with their " unfortunatee Captain. They gathered around the table, an foaming mugs of ale were freely quaffed, for "soirow's dry" they said. But neither laugh, song, nor jest attended their draughts. They were to keep that night's vigil in honour of their Captain, and then were to disband and separate for ever. Suddenly, in the midst of their heavy grief and utter silence, a familiar sound was heard—a ring- ing footstep under the back windows. And every man leaped to his feet, with looks Of wild delight and questioning. And the next instant the door wits flung wide open, and the outlaw chief stood among them! Now listen, lads, for I must talk fast 1 I have already lost a great deal too much time. I went first te the cave in the Punch Bowl, and not finding you there, came here at a venture, where I am happy to meet you for the last time—for to-Bight' we disband forever!" " 'Twas our intention, Captain," said Hal', in a melancholy voice. Black Donald than threw himself into a seat at the head of the table, poured out a mug of ale, and invited his band to pledge him. They gathered around the table, filled their mugs, pledged him standing, and then resumed their seats to listen to the last words of their chief. Black Donald commenced and related the man- ner of his deliverance by Capitola.; and then taking from his bosom a bag of gold, he poured it upon the table and divided it into two equal portions. one of which he handed to "Headlong Mal'," saying.— "There, Hal', take that and divide it among your companions, and scatter to distant parts of the country, where you may yet have a chance of earn- ing an honest livelihood As for me I shall have to quit the country altogether, and it will take nearly half this sum to enable me to do it. Now I shall have not a minute more to give you! So once more pledge your Captain and away 1" Toe men filled their mugs, rose to their feet, and pledged their leader in a parting toast, and then— " Good luck to you all!" exclaimed Black Donald, waving his hat thrice above his head with a vale- dictory hurrah. WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Conncil was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday evening. The Mayor (Councillor Fairclough) occupied the chair, and there were also present :—Aldermen Greenall, Harrison, Francomb,Sutton, Wright, Hutchin- son ; Councillors Plumpton, Duncan, Evans, G timer. Ellison, Baird, Lee, Roberts, Rogers, Shaw-Green, Tin- nion, Hepherd. Furness, Bennett, Woods, Smethurst, Monks, Cannell, Longton, Shaw, Shelton; and Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), T. Longdin (borough sur- veyor.)

THE STREIT IMPROVEMENT COMMITTER-IMPROVEMENT OF WILDERSPOOL CAUSEWAY.

A'derman Sterron, in moving the confirnaation of the minutes of this Committee, said the permission that the Committee proposed to give te Messrs. Bishop and Com- pany to erect a transporter at their new premises in Wharf-street, was part of the arrangement made with the Company in connection with the tenancy of the new wharf. With regard to the resolution " That the sum of £3O be paid to Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., Limited, for a strip of land and alterations to the White Bull Inn, Horsemarket-street ; this was with a view to setting back the lin of frontage from the Lan- cashire and Yorkshire Bank to the Griffin Inn, which would no doubt be considered an improvement. (Hear, hear.) A third resolution passed by the committee was "That in consideration of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Ce., Limited, giving up one of the rolling ways in front of the White Bull Inn, the Company be relieved from the tresspass rents paid in respect of the cellar areas in front of the Roe Buck Inn in Bridge Street." The committee considered that the arrangement would be beneficial to the town. Another resolution had refer- ence to the purchase of land from Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Company, Limited, for the purpose of widening Wilderspool Causeway to sixty feet. There had been for many years past, he (Alderman Sutton) said, many complaints about the narrowness and the bad condition of the road. Probably on no other road into Warrington had there been so much traffic as upon this and the difficulty of vehicles in passing and repairing caused an annoyance to residents and passen- gers, especially as on one side of the road there was no footpath at all. This traffic had no doubt been relieved to some extent by the new road across Arpley but the diminution of traffic had been scarcely apparent. It would be observed that the road was to be made sixty feet wide, but the Wilderepoel Brewery Company would give all the land that was necessary to make the road a uniform width of forty feet. Beyond forty feet and up to sixty feet the Corporation would have to pay for the land at the rate of 6a. per square yard. Under the cir- cumstances he considered that this was a very advisable purchase. The MAYOR: Some parts of the road are not thirty-five feet wide but the Brewery Company will make it forty feet, and we pay fcr the rest. Alderman SUTTON said that was so. The Committee considered the price to be paid under the circumstances was a very reasonable one. Councillor FURNESS seconded the adoption of the Com- mittee's minutes. Councillor SHAY/ called attention to several grids in Horsemarket-street—the entrance to cellars—which were in a slippery condition. The MAYOR pointed out that the subject was not on the minutes. Councillor Shaw should call tiae attention of the Borough Surveyor to the matter. Councillor SHAW-GREEN congratulated the committee on making the arrangements with regard to Wilder- spool Causeway and on having taken the steps they had done before any portion of the land was built upon. (Hear, hear.) Councillor ROBERTS also congratulated the committee on the bargain, because he considered that while they, were spending considerable amounts of money in widen- ing streets in the borough it was important that the en- trances to the town were wilened. If they let that matter rest any longer they would net have such an opportunity again for many years and they would cer- tainly not then get the land at the price at which they were now obtaining it. Although there was loom for two opinions as to the value of this at the present time, he must say that from the opinions he had heard expresssed of people who were in a position to know he thought the price a fair one, which they ought not to let pass them. (Hear, hear.) If they could only do the same thing for Knutsford-road he was sure they would be delighted to have the opportunity. (Hear, hear.) The minutes of the committee were then adopted. SANKEY AND HOWLEY BROOKS —THE SMALL PDX HOSPITAL. Councillor BENNETT a,lend the Chairman of the Health Committee the following question :—" Whether it is a fact that a memorial to the Local Government Board complaining of the S (nkey Brook and requesting them to interfere in the matter has been extensively signed by the inhabitants of the district and whether the commit- tee will take inallediate steps to compel the various authorities responsible for the nuisance to abate the same; also whether the Howley Brook is under the con- trol of the committee, and, if so, whether they are aware that it is becoming a mere repository for rubbish, and will consider the desirability ot filling in the portion con- tiguous to the Open Space at the back of Church-street." He remarked that the memorial was got up by Council- lor Plumpton and that it was only fair that he should have the crcdit of it. Alderman SUTTON, in reply, said the committee fully sympathised with the memorialists in this case, but they did not know how to deal with the thing. The nuisance was created at St. Helens and at other places. They were not responsible in Warrington for the nuisance. It came down to them and they were bound to accept it as it is, but at the same time he would promise Mr. Bennett that the committee would do all they possibly could to assist the memorialists in getting the Local Government Board to take stops in the matter in order to do away with the nuisance. In regard to the second portion ef the question he might say that Howley Brook was not under the control of the Health Committee. The Paving and Sewerage Committee had to do with this.

Councillor BENNATT said the Paving and Sewerage Committee had referred him to the Health Committee. Alderman Surrow repeated that the Paving and Sewer- age Committee had to do with the Brook. He had al- ways been under the impression that that Committee bad so far done all that could be done in regard to How- ley Brook. He then moved the adoption of the minutes of the Health Committee, and referring to the proposed small-pox hospital, he said the Committee were of opinion that thee would be no further delay. It was very necessary to proceed at once, for as most of them would be aware during the last week or so there had been a case of small-plx introduced into the town. He was pleased to tell them that the man was getting bet- ter and that there was now no danger of the disease spreading, yet the case showed the necessity of taking steps to provide themselves with a hospital. Councillor BENNETT asked the Mayor which Commit- tee was responsible for Howley Brook. The MAYOR : The Town Clerk says it belongs to the Paving and Sewerage Committee. Councillor ROBERTS said he should like to assure the

eople in the neighbourhood of Sankey Brook that the matter was not being lost sight of. The fact was that their difficulties in consequence of the offences of the neighbouring authorities were so great that they hardly knew which way to move first for the best, but the matter would be thoroughly dealt with as far as lay in their Dower and that quickly. With reference to Howley Brook, if there was any nuisance detrimental to health it should be dealt with by the Health Committee. As to the smallpox nobody need go away with the idea that there is anything to take fright at. They were always liable to the introduction of smallpox and they could trace this case pretty well to its source. The period of infection had now passed without the slightest infection, but the case was a reminder to the committee that no time should be lost in the erection of the hospital. (Hear, hear.) Alderman FRANCO3fB asked if anything fnrther had been done with regard to the old river at Wilderspool. Alderman SUTTON said the old river had become such a nuisance that he believed the best way to deal with it would be to fill it up. The minutes were then adopted. WAGES AT LONGFORD DEPOT.—THE DEPUTATION BLAMED.

Councillor MONKS moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Sanitary Works Committee which recom- mended "that the wages of the firemen and stokers at Longford Depot be ir creased by the sum of 23. each per week." In doing so he said he moved that resolution with mixed feelings. There was a moral in it which he would like to point out. The Sanitary Works Comm;t- tee gave an invitation to the Council to visit the Long- ford works and that resolution was the result of it. He thought it rather hard lines on the committee—or any committee—that people after visiting the com- mittee's works should then get up a sort of agita- tion amongst the men that they should make a request for higher wages. The committee never had a word of complaint from the men pnviously, and he (Councillor Monks) felt all the more about it because he did not think an increase of wages was the right thing to do. He thought it would have been better to have made a reduction in the working hours. The men now worked twelve hours a day, and it would have been much better to keep Mr e wages the same and an additional shift put on. He protested against the action of members of the Council in getting up an agitation amongst the men for additional wages as had been done in that particular case. Councillor EVANS seconded the adoption of the minutes.

Alderman SUTTON thouttht before passing a resolution the committee should make inquiries as to the rate of wages paid to tanners' labourersiand see how they com- pared with those paid to the men at Longford works. He did not think anyone was justified in creating dis- satisfaction amongst the workneople at Longford. The men should themselves be the first to complain. Unless the wages paid were lower than those paid to other labourers in the town he did not think it was a proper thing to increase them. Alderman HARRISON asked whether he was to under- stand that people who were comparative strangers to the work, had taken upon themselves to dictate to the committee?

Councillor MONK.; They did not dictate, but they euggested that the men should have an advance of wages. Alderman HARRISON said it seemed a most incredible affair that the committee should take notice of strangers and allow them to dictate—for he still held that it was dictating—the rate of wages to be paid. Councillor Saaw GRREN asked how many men were effected by the resolution, and was he to understand that when the deputation visited the depot, some mem- bers of the Council suggested that the men's wages were insufficient ?

The MAYOR: It was suggested in this chamber after- wards.

Councillor SHAW GREEN said if the resolution related to the men who did the" dollying " he did not wonder that they felt they wanted more wages. Councillor SMETHURST said the resolution had given the committee a considerable amount of trouble con- sidering that they had made their estimates for the year. But they had no choice in the matter. They had to give the men the increased wages or they would have ceased work, although they were getting 253. a week. From inquiries made in tanneries it was found that the la- bourers' wages varied from 163. to 181. per week. But at the same time the men at Longford deserved more remuneration than that owing to the character of the work.

Ceuncillor ROBERTS thought some members of the Council were taking too harsh a view of the matter. Some members of the Council sate the condition of things at the works and it certainly appealed to their humanity. There were members who considered that the exhairiting nature of the work and the disagreeable and disgusting character of it was such as to entitle the men to very fair consideration in the shape of wages. If the committee had felt that their hands had been forced in the matter, they need not be unduly sensitive in re- gard to it because the object was a good one and a pro- per one. It was far better for the Corporation to err on the side of liberality rather than to be accused of keep- ing their men down, which under the circumstances. would hardly be creditable to a municipality like theirs He did not know a single trade where they could com- pare the nature of the work with that done at Longford. He did not think the increase of wages was a wrong step for the committee to take, and he should be glad if in addition they could see their we} to start the eight hours shift. (lear, hear.) When they came to con- sider what twelve hours a day meant in front of those furnaces at Longford, and the circumstances of the men, he did not think 273. a week would land them in Para- dise all in a hurry. (Laughter.) Councillor SHAW said he was one of the deputation to Longford, and it was quite possible that he might be the father of the suggestion of the increase of 2s. a woek. He did make inquiries at the time as to the wages paid,and he told the Chairman of the co:nmittee that he did not think it sufficient money or the disagreeable work they had to do. It was quite possible that some- one had heard him say so, but he denied that he had suggested it to the manager or the workmen. If he had done he should not feel ashamed of it, for he thought the men richly deserved the increase in wages. Councillor MONKS, in reply, said he did not think it should hove been suggested to the men that they should apply for an advance of wages. It was not on account of hard work that the increase of wages had been given. The firemen at the furnaces were not effected by the resolution, but the increase was being given to the four or sis men who were practically unskilled labourers. The men had been there for some years, and the com- mittee did not like to run the risk of turnine them out and engaging another set of men. They would now get 275. per week. The MAYOR : What did they start at ? Councillor MONKS : 25s.--Continuing, he said there was nothing more objectionable at Longford than in some places, and it certainly was not dangerous. The committee were endeavouring to improve matters and they had in view alterations which had been suggested, which if succeseful, would remove altogether the slightest possibility of any smell or nuisance in any form whatever. (Hear, hear.) That would practically remove the arguments which some of the members had made in favour of the wages being in- creased.

The minutes were then passed. PAVING AND SEWERAGE COMMITTEE.

Alderman7W-e—mn—r--moVed the confirmation of these minutes, and observed that during the past month ac- counts amounting to £2,740 had been paid, and con- tracts accented with regard to various streets to the ex- tent of £1,425 so that it would be seen the committee were not quite idle, (Hear, hear.) He then read a report of the Borough Surveyor which stated that "the 'laving of Latchford-road has been completed for about 900 vards, and we are extending the same to opposite Grove-street, Bowers-street, Jones's-buildings. The adjoining passages off Winwick-road are now completed,as well as the paving of Stevenson-street. The passages bounded by Sankey-street, Parker-street, and Museum-street are well in hand. and I expect will be completed this week. New-street and Gibson-street at Howley have been commenced,and I expect in the course of a month will be completed. The wood paving in Sankey-street I propose to commence on Monday next, while that in Palmyra-square is completed. We are now engaged in carrying out the drainage scheme from Pad- gate joining with the storm overflow sewer in Napier- street.and expect that it will be finished this week. The wharf is progressing satisfactorily, and I hope before your next meeting we shall be engaged in forming the approach road to it. I have examined the new road across Arpley,and find that it is standing very well. All other works are proceeding satisfactorily. Bishop's Wharf nearly completed."—Alluding to Howley Brook, Alderman WRIGHT said nothing went into it but surface water—drainage from the land. He had been to the place that day, and could see nothing that could be deemed to be a nuisance. They could not carry on the permanent sewers for the present, but during the next three or four months the whole land would be laid out, and they would then lay down permanent drains. Councillor BAIRD seconded the passing of the minutes. Alderman FRANCOMP complained of the way the gullies were cleaned out, but the Mayor said that the matter was not on the minutes. Alderman Wright could no doubt put the matter right. Councillor LONGTON was pleased to hear the report of the Borough Surveyor read. The MAYOR: The Alderman was out of order all the time he was reading it. (Laughter.) TIIE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 15, 1896.

DESTRUCTIVE FIRE AT WARRINGTON.

EXCITING SCENES. -NA RROW ESCAPES.

On Friday morning last shortly after midnight an extensive fire was found to have broken out on the premises of Mr. Thomas Daiutith, gunsmith and en- graver. of 82, Bridge-street, Warrington. P.C. Hardy was the first to discover the fire. At a quarter past midnight he was standing at the top of Rylands-street when he perceived a smell of burning rubber and he set out to discover the origin. He proceeded to Mr. Daintith's shop and on going down the entry close to, he saw that the workshop and storerooms ot Mr. Daintith situated at the back of the premises, were on fire and that the flames were gaining a good hold on the building. He knocked at the door with his truncheon but finding that he was not heard, he threw himself on the door and burst it open. After giving the alarm to the inmates he immediately proceeded to the Market Place and rang the fire bell, and afterwards assisted to get out the fire engine.

In the meantime there were some exciting scenes at the burning premises. The workshop and store- rooms, as already stated, are at the back of the premises, and above them is a large kitchen. On the third storey are a number of bedrooms and in these some of the children were sleeping as well as a domestic servant. In a room,a little further back, an aged woman, 82 years of age, named Mrs. Ains- worth, was also sleeping at the time of the con- flagration. On being hastily awakened they found themselves enveloped in flame and smoke and rushed out of the room in their nightdresses. Two daughters of Mr. Daintith The Misses Eva and Nellie Daintith were in a most perilous position for immediately after they had left their bedroom the floor gave way and fell with a crash. They succeeded in descending a staircase and with assistance reached the Lion Hotel, just opposite the shop. Mrs. Ainsworth found it impossible to leave her room by the staircase, and the aged woman was obliged to get through the window. She clung to the window-sill until grasped by Mr. Daintith, but both fell to the ground and the woman was severely shaken. She was the last of the nine occupants to leave the burning building. Sergeant Hollingworth having arrived on the scene, araused the people living in close proximity, and there was speedily an animated spectacle.

Within a short period after the sounding of the fire-bell, Supt. Turner with deputy Supt. F. 11. Turner, engineer Ratcliffe, and eleven firemen, proceeded with the new fire engine "Captain," to the conflagration. On reaching Market Gate, tongues of flame and smoke shooting above the burning building glared in the faces of the firemen. Efforts were chiefly made on arrival to save the contiguous property from disaster, and the manner in which the men went about their work was favourably commented upon. A fair supply of water was obtained but there was not sufficient to find work for the " Major," and this engine was, therefore, not brought into use. The fire 'irigade from Wilderspool also arrived on the scene,but their services were not required. Unfortunately for Mr. Daintith, he had a large stock of bicycles an ammunition on his premises at the time, and it is computed that over eighty pounds of gunpowder went off during the confla- gration, and as will be readily imagined added not a little to the flame. Some stray shots from the car.ridges struck several who were working at the fire. Superintendent Turner was struck on the forehead, and Sergeant Hollingworth was also struck rather violently on the breast with a bullet. A number of bicycles—about a score—were saved, but as many more were included in the general wreckage of the building. Willing hands, includ- ing several members of the Corporation, aided in rescuing goods from the front shop, and also the books. One constable at considerable risk, pro- ceeded to one of the rooms and fetched Mr. Dain- tith's cash-box. It was not until three o'clock that the fire was got under, and the firemen are to be congratulated npon their success in confining the flames to the building in which they originated. Great praise is also due to the Chief Constable and his staff for the great services they rendered. The premises belong to the Warrington Corporation and the damage is covered by insurance. Mr. Daintith had intended to remove in a few days to the shop at Bridge Foot, previously occupiad by Mr. Marsh in consequence of the improvement which the Cor- poration intend to make in the widening of the street. The fire has robbed him of almost the whole of his stock, his furniture and tools, in addition to a number of bicycles which he had in hand for repair. Only the bare walls of the building have been left standing and these are in a very unsafe condition.

The damage sustained by Mr. Daintith is esti- mated at over .£lOOO, and is only partially insured in the West of England Assurance Company, now known as the Commercial Union Assurance Com- pany, Limited, of which Messrs. Jenkins and Com- pany ara the local agents. The origin of the tire is not known.

===== 0002601_18960815_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
STATEMENT BY AN EYE-WITNESS.

One of the eye-witnesses of .the fire was Mr. Edward Pickavance, son of Mr. Pickavance, leather merchant of Bridge-street,whose premises are almost opposite to those of Mr. Daintith, and in answer to inquiries, he, told our reporter that he was first made aware of the fire by the screams of the young women who fortunately escaped from the burning building. On going to the place he saw the flames bursting through the windows of the back premises, and immediately afterwards they shot out to the skies with fearful force and fury, due to some extent to the stock of powder in the building. The light, he remarked, wee as brilliant as an electric light, and illuminated the surrounding buildings and streets for some dis- tance. In company with a number of others, he set to work to save some of the goods in the estab- lishment, and amongst those who ren- dered assistance, he noticed Alderman Wright, and Conncillor Woods. Mr. Pickavance was instrumental in saving the books of Mr. Daintith, which he took to his father's shop for safety, to- gether with a quantity of revolvers, guns, etc. Some of the stock from the front shop was also taken to a storeroom at the Lion Hotel. Both Nfr. Edward Pickavance and his father were unstinted in their praises of the efforts of the firemen who although hampered by want of space, worked zeal- ously from start to finish, and prevented what would have been a dire calamity if the flames had spread to the adjoining premises.

THE POLICE REPORT. P.C.Joseph Hardy reported that he was on duty at 12.15 a.m.onFriday in Bridge-street when he smelled something burning and went higher up Bridge- street. He then found the workshop of Mr. Thomas Daintith, gunsmith and cycle agent, 82, Bridge-st., on fire. Knowing that the family lived on the prerniss he knocked at the Goor and with some difficulty aroused the inmates. He then rang the fire bell and went to the Fire Station to help to get the engine out. The firemen were knocked up by P.C.'s 15, 40, and 50. In the meantime P.S. Ilollingworth went to the fire and got the people out of the houses in Bridge- street buildings and they were removed Ship Sh Yard. Mr. and Mrs. Daintith and family, eight i in number, were got to the Lion Hotel. A Mrs. Eliza- beth Ainsworth, 80 years of age, who lived n a room over the workshop was got out of the room window and taken to the l i hotel n her nightdrevs. ' The shop was entered by the police and a number of men helped to get the stock of guns and other con- tents into the vaults of the Lion Hotel and put in safety. P.C. Bradley went into the top storey of the shop by means of a ladder and got the cash box, containing over £lOO, and a watch and quantity of clothes. Detective Barlow, nine constables and a number of civilians also assisted:Me works were left all safe at 11.30 p.m.by the son,Mr. 11. Daintith and they all retired to rest about midnight. The origin of the fire is not known at present or the amount of i damage done. It s partly insured. The report concludes by stating that there were at the fire the Chief Constable, Inspector Cox, Detectives Barlow and Roper, two sergeants and twelve constables. They were engaged at thp fire from 12.20 a.m. to 4 Om.

The minutes ware passed. THE OFFENSIVE CONDITION OF THE SHIP CANAL AND RIVER. _ _ .

Councillor ROBERTS, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Rivers' Committee alluded to the ono in which the attention of the M Tsey and I rwell Joint Com- mittee was called to the filthy condition of the Ship Canal near Warrington, and the old portion of the river Mersey. He said the committee had called attention to the matter, and he was afraid that that was all they could do at present. Alderman HARRISON : I am afraid it is. Councillor ROBERTS continuing, said that the Corpora- tion were only contributors to the offensive condition of the old river in a very slight degree ; and this contribu- tion was to be taken out at the earliest possible moment by the drainage scheme which was in hand. It would be taken into the tidal por'ion of the river, away from the possibility of doing any harm, so that until the Mersey and Irwell Joint Committee had compelled all contributing authorities higher u the river to withhold rendering the whole stream foul and offensive, he was afraid that the Corporation would have to wait a con- eiderable period before the present state of things was remedied. During the next few months, however, the penstock which the Ship Canal Company were con- structing would afford a considerable improvement. The people who lived on the banks were to be commiser- ated at the present time, but he could assure them that so far as they had any power it would be used to the fullest possible extent in compelling those whom they could compel to make the condition of this part of the river and Ship Canal as little offensive as possible. The Joint Committee were proceeding against the Manches- ter Corporation, and something like 50 firms and auth- orities for committing offences in this respect, and there was some hope that within A reasonable time, the general conditions would be improved. Counrillor FIEPFIERD serended. ACKNOWLEDGEMENT OF COUNCILLOR ROBERTS' SERVICES. The MAYOR said he wished to propose a vote of thanks to Counillor Roberts for the time and trouble he had taken in looking after the interests of Warrington in the na.gotiations in connection with the Ship Canal. (Hear, hear.) The least the Corperation could do was to accord him a hearty vote of tha.nke,and he had pleasure in sub- initting such a resolution. (Hear, hear.) Alderman %Veronr seconded the motion, observing that it would be impossible for anyone to have paid more attention to the interests of Warrington than Councillor Roberts had done. (Hear, hear.) The motion was unanimously agreed to. Councillor ROBERTS, in responding, said the resolution had come as a complete surprise. All the same he thanked the Mayor most cordially for such a generous testimony to what he had tried to do in the best in- terests of Warrinatnn. and the members of the Council for so cordially adopting the proposition. It was a matter of pride to him to be connected with the Cor- poration. He was a native of Warrineton, and ha loved the old town quite as much as if he were the most ardent Conservative in it—(laughter)—and he was bound to say that when the interests of Warrington were con- cerned it had been a matter of great pleasure to him to receive the cordial support of those who on other mat- ters did not think with him. Everything else had been dropped where the interests of Warrington were con- cerned; and they had worked in this matter more par- ticularly as thoroughly and harmoniously as nossible. He hoped to be allowed to live a few years yet to be of seryice to Warrington. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

THE JUBILEE OF THE CORPORATION.

The minutes of the General Purposes Committee con- taining the following suggestions submitted'by the com- mittee appointed to consider anzi report upon the arrangements to be made for celebrating the Jubilee of the Corporation, were considered and accepted for fur- ther consideration :--1, to hold an art and industrial ex- hibition ; 2, to beat the bounds of the extended borough by a procession of the inhabitants ; 3, to'arran ge a treat for school children; 4, to arrange a treat for the aged poor 5, to cast a commemoration medal • 6, to plant trees in all suitable streets and places ;7, to confer the freedom of the borough upon certain inhabitants for eminent service rendered to the borough.

(Hear, hear.) He stated that the industrial exhibition wag first suggested by the Chamber of Commerce, as it was be- lieved that it would tend to show what the town was oapable ef doing in the shape of production, as well as he a benefit to local trade by advertising their capacity in almost every field of industrial labour. It was also thought that it might form the nucleus of a nermanent collection. In many towns on the Continent there were industrial or commercial in.:mums of immense value to the manufacturers in the neiehhourhood, and it had occurrei to the Warrington Chamber of Com- merce that the interests of the Warrington Museum would be augmented if they had a Com- mercial Depertment, in which all their leading local manufacturers were properly represented. He was pleased with the proposal to plant treesin various streets of the borough. He had to make a further suggeetion, that tablets should be placed on some of the old build- ings in Warrington. There were many houses in town, which they passed almost every day of their lives in which people of great eminence in ecience and literature and art once resided. They should realise that in the midst of this smoke and hum of commerce the town had not been quite dead to the higher ideals, and that the town had been the birthplace of some very distinguished men. These tablets would be a proner commemoration of the jubilee of the.Cerpora.tion. The most interesting place of all mobster was the old house at Bridle Fool., which was the original Academv.and which, he thought reflected more glory upon Warrington than anything else. THE MURDER OF MRS. KANE,

OF WARRINGTON.

DOUBLE EXECUTION AT NOTiINGIEAM.

CONFESSION BY WILKINSON.

Samuel Wilkinson, aged forty-eight, gardener, and John Rose, fifty-three, baker, were executed at Notting- ham on Tuesday, the former for murdering Mrs. Kaye, an elderly lady, at Arnold, and the latter for cutting his wife's throat. In response to private representations, the Home Secretary intimated a few days ago that he saw no reason for advising her Majesty to interfere with the due carrying out of the law, and the culprits upon being informed of the date axed for the execution dis- played little concern; but paid most careful attention to the ministrations of the prison chaplain, and appeared thoroughly contrite. _ _

_ On Monday, after partaking of Holy Communion, Wilkinson made a complete confession of his crime, but Rose made no statement. Both were on Tuesday morn- ing early visited by the chaplain, who spent a long time with each. The culprits walked firmly to the scaffold, and death was instantaneous,Billington being the execu- tioner, assisted by Wilkinson,of Bolton. Reporters were cot admitted, but they were subsequently informed that the arrangements were carried out satisfactorily and with expedition. John Rose had murdered his wife, having cut her throat whilst she lay in bed. He attempted to commit suicide, inflicting a severe wound in his throat, but he recovered. Rose was visited last week by his brother and sister, and Wilkinson was seen by his wife and two brothers, the farewell intervals being of an affecting character.

===== 0002601_18960815_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
WILKINSON'S CRIME.

Mrs. Kaye, who was murdered by Wilkinson, was a Warrington woman,and resided with her daughter, Mrs. Morrall, in Bewsey-road. Her son formerly carried on business in Markey-street as a hairdresser and elsewhere in the town. Immediately on the receipt of the news Mrs. Morrall proceeded to Nottingham General Hospital. The surprise of the relatives may be imagined when they received information of the terrible maltreatment the old lady had received. In October last she went to Not- tingham to stay with a married son, who is an engineer on the Great Northern Railway, intending to return to Warrington in March. During her stay in Nottingham, however, Mrs. Kaye accepted an invitation to spend a few days at the house of Mr. Samuel Powley, a market gardener, living at Arnold. Mr. Powley, who lives in Gedling-lane, was one of the eight zandidates seeking election on the Arnold Urban District Council, and on Friday night, February 14th. was busily engaged can- vassing in the district,and in the course of his busineas happened to go in the Ram Inn. During his stay there a stranger entered, and seeing Mr. Powley, asked a man sitting near who he was. His neighbour answered that it was Mr. Powley, a market gardener. Shortly afterwards Mr. Powley left the house, being followed by the stranger, who eventually found his way to Mr. Powley's house. Mrs. Powley and the children, together with Mrs. Kaye, were in the house when a knock came to the door. Before they could get there the stranger,who was described as a thin, dark man, rather tall, and attired in a hard felt hat and black suit entered. After asking if Mr. Powley lived there, and being answered in the affirmative, the stranger volunteered the information that Mr. Powley had met with a very serious accident by being run over by a bicycle, and was lying badly hurt at the Ram Inn, where he wished to see his wile and children. Mrs. Fawley was naturally greatly alarmed, and went out with the stranger, one of her sons going ahead, leaving Mrs. Kaye in the house. The stranger, after accom- panying Mrs. Powley some distance into the town, left her after wishing " Good-night," and she proceeded on to the Ram Inn by herself. She had not gone far when she was met by her son, who informed her that his father was all right. Suspecting that there was something wrong, she told the lad to go for a policeman and proceeded home. On arrival at Mr. Powley's house, which is quite isolated, being at least three hundiad yards from any other dwelling house, a dreadful discovery met their eyes. Mrs. Kaye was found lying in the back kitchen in a pool of blood. Her head had been terribly battered with a kitchen poker, which was found afterwards ben!, and indented. The unfortunate woman lay on her back, moaning slightly. The ceiling and three of the walls were covered with blood, being evidence of the brave resistance made by the old laly to the miscreant's attack. About the room were traces of a struggle hav- ing taken place, and from what can be gathered it would appear that the old lady was suddenly surprised by the thief, but with coolness and presence of mind re- markable in a woman of such advanoed years, seized him and endeavolared to eject him. The man, made an attempt to escape, but bars. Kaye held on to him and endeavoured to atttact assistance by screaming. Poss- ibly had it not been for this brave attempt to detain the man Mrs. Kaye would not have been injured. The thief evidently apprehending that outside attention would soon be attracted became violent with the woman, dragged her into the room, and as could be seen from what was found there afterwards, made the most brutal and murderous attack upon her, using the p ker with such force as to cause her death. He then carried out his object of robbing the house.

===== 0002601_18960815_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR I. T. BRUNNER AND THE SCHOOL BOARD QUESTION AT BARNTON.

A parish meeting was held at Barnton, near North- wich,on Monday evening, for the purpose of discussing the School Board question.—SirJ.T.Brunner was present, and appealed to the ratepayers not to accept the Bishop's offer to enlarge the present school, which is without a playground. He promised to give half an acre of ground and £5OO towards a Board school, end to pay sufficient in the first year to bring the rate down to 31. If the ap- peal made to the Education Department ended in favour of the Bishop,he did not mean to relax his efforts to per- suade them to have a good school of their own, and not a clergyman's school, and he would back the people's school against that of the Bishop and clergy. If the worst came to the worst, and the Bishop persuaded the Government not to give a grant to the new Board school, on the ground that it was usnecessary, then he would pay the grant himself until they got a Govren- ment which would give them a grant. (Cheers•)--A. resolution was almost unanimously carried that an ap- plication should be made to the Education Department for the formation of a School Board.—A poll was de- manded.

The result is the sudden develor ment of this Cretan question, the seriousneo of which cannot be exaggerated.
