to this, that if any compulsion is to be used it must be applied against the Sultan as well as against his insurgent subjects. The Sultan is, if the British Government has its way, to be com- pelled to meat the wishes of the Cretans to a reasonable extent, and, what is more, he is4to7give satisfactory guarantees that his promises of better government will be faithfully carried out. This Would, in effect, mean the granting of self-govern- ment to Crete, for there could be no satisfactory guarantee of good government as long as the Pashas of Constantinople were allowed to inter- fere with local administration. It is to be hoped that the other Powers of Europe will see the wis- dom of the English plan of dealing with Turkish troubles. It is a plan which could be applied as Occasion required to other disturbed portions of Turkey, and it seems to offer the only alternative to that violent break-up of the Ottoman Empire Ivilioh may lead to a disastrous European war.

THE "national game of cricket " Cricket. has held a truly national place in the minds of the people this week. This is mainly due to two causes—one being the rubber match between the England and Australian teams, the other being the "strike of the profeb- eionals." The Australian cricketers have done Wonderfully well in England. When there names Were first announced there were not a few critics 41 this country who wrote in a disparaging fashion ef their capabilities as batsmen ar..d predicted that the tour in England would be,if not a failure, at least a very inoified success. But these critics have had the doubtful satisfaction of seeing all their kournful forecasts completely falisfied. The Aus- tralians have, as cricketers, been a great success. "they have defeated every English county that has played against them, and their vic- tories have in many instances been of of a very pronounced character. They were de- feated in the first of the" England v. Australia" klatches, but in the second they won a very cre- ditable success. The intense interest that was taken in the final match this week was in itself a high compliment to our visitors. It showed that the result of the match was by no means taken Or granted, although England was to be repre- Icated by the best.team of ccricketers that could be got together in this country. This fact in itself 4Poke volumes for the formidable reputation Which the Australian players have established. /'ortunately the result of the match still leaves the supremacy in cricket in the charge a the liother Country, but had the Australians won 40 one could have said that their success was due tO a" fluke." It would simply have been the crowning incident in a particularly successful,tour. the" strike of the professionals," as it has been termed, was in one respect a splendid advertise- Nett for the great match, while it did no harm so far as the selection of players was concerned, as The " strike " was at an end before the team was Anally arranged. It, arose through the profes- 4ionalk of the Surrey Club asking for the fee of t2O each for playing at the great match in place .14 £lO which has been the usual payment. There lei it appears, a good deal to be said on behalf of the claims of the professional cricketers. The game le only played about four months in the year, and .8a they have to pay all their own expenses incur- teql in travelling and in other ways, it is said that oeo the best amongst them barely earn sufficient to keep them the year round. Their earnings cer- tainly fall a long way short of those of some other Professional caterers for public amusement, and they are even very much less, it is said, than what are secured by distinguished amateurs under the head of "expenses." , Yet the amateur ,has to be SatretullY described in the cricket reports as " Mr." while the %ere professional is only known by his surname, which is• an offensive form of snobbish- Ass. The amateur )1? 05 not disdain to pocket his expenses has lull access to the pavilion arid its Drivileges whilst thehrunble " pro " is carefully exl- --'l4cled as though he were an inferior mortal. Yet the professionals who have made most of the 554, aty teams what they are, and it is their skilfull ,13143 P which draws the " gates " from which the 4411ds are drawn. On the whole, therefore, it 4eenas evident that these professional cricketers have real grievances which should be remedied. As 11.11 example of the contrasts in payment it is said, Or instance, that as a result of ,the match of this Week the Austrailian players will probably receive eoreething like 210 Q each, while the two English Professional bowlers, Peele and Hearne, who really Won the matAwill receive £lO each and out of this ellni they will have to pay their own 'railway fares ,10. and from London and their aw n expenses whilst 111' the capital. There is here, surely, a glaring in-,. equality in the distribution of the profits. It is a 'reversal of the old maxim of the spoils to the *isotor."

BILLIARD LEAGUE.

A billiard league amongst the Cheshire Liberal clubs is about to be formed and the composition of the league will probably include such towns as Runcorn, North- wich, Knutsford, Nantwich, Crewe, Warrington, Da*fen- ham, Altrincham, and Chester.

Frith was the holder, of the lucky number drawn, and she accordingly became entitled to £2OO free of interest for 121 years. ELLESMERE-STREET SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN.

The scholars attending this schoolopent Monday after- nocn on the hills at Overton. During the day a substan- tial tea was provided, the teachers at the school presid- ing at the tables. A return was made about nine o'clock, Runcorn being reached after a pleasant day's enjoy- ment.

PRESENTATION TO MR. W. H. CHADWICK, RUNCORN. On Tuesday evening Mr. W. H. Chadwick was pre- sented with a large steel engraving framed in oak and gold, as a mark of recognition of his services for many years in connection with the superintendency of Bethesda Congregational Sunday School. Miss Richardson and Miss Johnson introduced by the pastor (the Rev. J. Simpson) made the presentation.

RUNCORN CYCLING CLUB RUNS.

On Saturday, that days section journeyed to Chelford, near Macclesfield the route taken being through Knuts- ford. The Wednesday section travelled to Hoylake.now famous for its golf links.—To lend variety to the run the journey was made via Liverpool while in returning the members passed throug Heswall and Chester. SALTATION ARMY TREAT AT RUNCORN.

On Thursday, 360 poor children were provided with tea in the Salvation Army Barracks, Runcorn, the occa- sion being the, termination of Lieut. Urquhart and Cap- tain Sherlocks stay in Runcorn. Before the tea a march out was held, and a meeting took place after the tea. The proceedings were very successful throughout. The funds were provided by subscriptions from different townspeople.

_ _ WALKING MATCH AT RUNCORN. On Wedeesday afternoon, a long pending walking match took place between Mr. W. Bankes, hairdresser, Runcorn, and Mr. R. Richardson. The course selected was from Halton Brook to the Red Lion Hotel, Moore., and Bankes going rapidly, completed the distance in 36, minutes. Richardson gave up the contest in the earlier. stage so that Bankes was returned as winner. VOLUNTEER CHURCH PARADE AT RUNCORN.

The members of the Runcorn Companies of the Ches.: hire Volunteer Regiment assembled 4 t their headquarters in Greenway-road on Sunday morning, and proceedcd to the Parish Church, were a sermon whs preached by the Rev. Alan Williams of the Mersey Mission to Seamen Liverpool. There were on parade:—Captains Harrissin, Thompson, and G. F. Ashton, Lieut. Brandrit, Quarter- master Sergeant flankinsop!, eight sergeants and 70 rank and file.

PRESENTATION TO A RUNCORN MANAGER.

Mr. Callender, on leaving the Riincorn Soap and Alkali Co.'s works, of which he has been manager for tile last few years, was presented with a malacca walk- ing stick, Older mounted, containing a suitable inscrip- tion,fa gold pendant, and silver serviette rings for Mrs: Callender. Reference was made at the presentation ceremony to the kindly manner in which Mr. Callender had carried out his duties since he had been manager of the works, and the way it had been appreciated by the men.. It may be interesting to note that Mr. Callender leaves Runcorn for Constamtinople, where he goes to undertake the managership of a smokeless cordite manufactory.

MISHAP TO A RUNCORN RIVER PILOT.

On Tiesdav afternoon, a rather curious accident befel Captain H. Hill of illuncorn, a well-known river pilot. The Captain wag on thl pierhead and about to step on a steamer, when Joe of the ropes " surged au.l caught him by the legs, thereby flinging him into the tidal basin under the stern of the steamer: The Captain being a sturdy swimmer immediately struck out for a ladder built in the wall which he soon reached and was able to climb on to the pier. Captain Hill quickly went home and having procured a change of clothing he proceeded to the mouth of the river with the belated steamer. The danger most feared by the bystanders was that the scraw might begin to work and cut the captain to pieces but very fortunately this did not happen.

A VIOLENT &Aiwa AT RUNCORN

On Wednesday morninv,,at the Runcorn Police Court, before Mesqrs. F. J. Norman and S. Taylor, C.C., a sea- man, answering the name of Robert Leonard, was charged with being drunk and disorderly on Tuesday night and tefusing to quit the licensed premises in Church-street known as the Lord Rodney. From the evidence given it was gathered that prisoner entered the licensed premises and asked to be supplied•with drink. On being refused he became very violent,ran the daugh- ter from behind the bar and threw two or three bottles after her along the lobby. P.C. Kendrick, who was called in, said the man was very violent and refused to leave the house and he had to take prisoner into custody. A fine of 20s. and 54. 441. costs was imposed. .

MARVELLOUS ESCAPE FROM DEATH A-T RUNCORN.

On Monday afternoon Henry Prophet, a Winsford lad, aged 10 years, met with a serious accident under the fol- lowing circumstances. He, with several children, was playing on Beacon Hill, Runcorn, and had climbed the highest point of the hill overh&oging a disused quarry. While there the lad seems to have lost his balance as he fell a distance of 30 feet into the quarry. Two men who witnessed the accident/pinkly went,to the lad's assist- ance and'thay were surprised to find him alive, though bleeding from a wound in his head. Having been carried to his grandmother's residence,at 18,Picow-street,he was afterwards examined by Dr. Robinson's assistant, who found that he had sustaiaed a severe bruise to his head, and that the back was badly bruised besides general 'shock to the systeni. No bones were broken.

FUNERAL AT RUNCORN.

The funeral of the body of Mr. James Dutton took place on Tuesday afternoen in the ,Ruacorn Cemetery. Mr. Dutton 'died last Saturday at his residence in Victoria-road at the age of 72 years. Formerly he carried on business in the town as a slate manufacturer but of late he had been in business as an undertaker &c. De- ceased was a prominent Wesleyan, a staunch teetotaler, 'and a Liberal . and as such was greatly respected. The Rev. T. THEFT BY A SAILOR AT RUNCORN.

On Friday merning at the Runcorn Police Court,before Messrs. S. Taylor, C.C.,and F.. 7. Norman, Enoch Leach, a seaman, was charged with, stealing a pair, of sea boots valued 183., the property of. Arnold Unswortb, on Wed- nesday night. Arnold Unsworth, bootand shoe-maker, Percival-lane, said that on Wednesday night, primmer and another man called at his place after ten o'clock, and they were shown some boots. The sea boots were amongst the goods shown. The left about ten minutes to eleven, and next morning when he straightened his shop no he missed the boots. He went to the docks and saw defendant and asked him for the Wellington boots he had taken. Detendant hod a box in his hand which he unlocked and gave witness his beets back.—P.Q. Wylie stated that on Thursday morning be was called to Mr. Unsworth's shop where he found the prisoner, and Mr. Unsworth said he had stolen a pair, of boots. He brought him down to the station and charged him with stealing the articles.—Prisoner pleaded guilty and.rhad nothing to say except that he was very sorry. It was the first time he had been in a police court. Prisoner was fined 203. or in default 14 cloys', imprisonment. —At the same court Thomas Fisher, an old man, of Batford, was charged with wandering abroad at Runcorn and soliciting alms. Prisoner pleaded guilty but was dis- charged en promising to clear out of the town. MARRIAGE OF TUE REV. Da. PRESTON'S SON. A quiet but very pretty wedding took place on Wed- nesday, when Mr. T. W. D. Preston, L.R.C.P., of Colin Green House, Mosborough, Derhystaire,the only son of the Rev. W. Preston, D.D., vicar of Holy Trinity. Runcorn, and nephew of the late Archdeacon Blackeney, vicar of Sheffield, and Miss Liliaft Mary Derbyshire, second daughter of the Rev. B. Derbyshire, MA., f Grassendale„were married. The ceremony was performed by the father of the bridegroom, assisted by the Rev. C. Yeid, M.A., vicar of Grassendale, at St Mary's Church, Grassendale. The bride, who looked very pretty, was given away by her father. She wore a smart gown of fawn canvas over pink silk, with bodice of embroidered grass lawn and empire sash and belt with large hat to match, and she carried a magnificent shower bouquet of white roses, lilies, and white heather, the gift of the bridegroom. There was one bridesmaid in attendance. Miss Ivy Dirbyshire, the bride's youngest eister, who wore a white muslin frock, with large white hat trimmed with feathers and pink roses, and carried a nosegay of pink .sweet pea; she also wore a watch, the gift of the bridegroom. The best man was Mr. W. Leete Derbyshire. Mendelssohn's Wedding March was played on the organ as the party were leavine the church. After the ceremony Mr. and Mrs. Derbyshire entertained the bridal party, which nembered forty,at their residence, King's Lea„and later in the afternoen,anaid showers of good wishes,the happy couple left for North Wales. The presents which num- bered over 100, were most costly.

Lord Armstrong, who is to entertain Li Hhung Cang next week, will have a good deal to show his visitor. His palatial residence at Cragside, with its ingenious electric lighting and other internal arrangements, will prove what can be done in a British country house. But the Chinese statesman will, no doubt,, had more to in- terest him in the neighbouring Itlswick works, for was not this the birthplace of several of those Japanese fight- ing craft which proved only too efficient at the mouth of the Yalu River and elsewhere ?

MYSTERIOUS A'FFAIR ON LIVERPOOL LIVERPOOL

EXPRESS TRAIN.

A fatal, and, as yet, mysterious occurrence took place on the Liverpool to Hull express, 3.45 from Liverpool; Central, on Friday afternoon, last week. As the express' at full speed approached the Widnes East Junction, near Farnworth, the driver of the Midland goods train which was approaching from the other direction, observed just as he came abreast of the express, a man fall out of a earflap door on the permanent way. He stoppe,ci his train and shouted to the signalman, who at once 'ob- served a man lying between the two seta of metals. He wired to Sankey to stop and examine the train, and in t e meantime he and the driver and guard of the goods train went up to the man, whom they found terribly cut about the head and otherwise mutilated. He was taken to the signal box, and Dr. O'Neill, Farnworth,sent for, but the injured man only lived about half an hour. From papers in his possession,it was found that his name was John Riley. he was apparently a seafaring man,, well-built, and of middle age. He had a ticket from Liverpool to Warrington. The ,body was conveyed to the Horn Inn, Cuerdley. In accordance with the stop and examine signal the express train was pulled up at Sankey, when it was' found that the carriage door was open, and that there was another passenger, a Warrington man, in the com- partment. The door was closed,and the train proceeded- but at Warrington the man was questioned. He said he was asleep at the time the occurrence happened, and he knew nothing about it. He did not know deceased. THE INQUEST. An inquest was held on the body by Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy-coroner, at the Horns Inn, Farnworth, on Mon- day afternoon-. Mr: William Gibson was foreman of the jury, and Mr. Simpson appeared' on behalf of the. Rail- way Company. Johanna Riley deposed that she lived at Calvert's-lane, Audenshaw, near Guide Bridge, Lancashire, and the deceased was her husband. He had worked at Garside's Brewery, Ashton-under-Lyne, for 16 years, but he had not worked anywhere for three years, she having kept him. He was 51. years of age. She last saw him alive at home a week last Saturday when he went te he 'sis- ter's and then to Liverpool, and took his papers with him to try and find employment. They lived very happily together and could not have been more ccm- fortable. Deceased had never threatened to take his Joseph Coyle, ship's cook, 112, Victoria-square. off Scotland-road, Liverpool, said he had known the deceased for a number of years, but had not seen him for a long period, until last Thursday. He then told witness that he had walked from Manchester to Liver- pool, arid had sat on the Custom House steps, Liverpool, for two nights. Witness and his wife gave deceased 3s. to take him back home. He left to go the Central Station, and at that time seemed down-hearted. E I cvard Slight stated that he was the guard of the 345 express from Liverpool to Hull on Friday., The train stopped at Garston and the carriages were then all right. The carriage occupied by the deceased was the third from his van. The first thing that drew hi A at- tention to something being wrong was when stopped by signal at Sinkey Station. when he found that a door was opened on the six-foot side. The window was down, and the door onlv opened from the outside. There was a man lying asleep in the carriage, whom he had since as- certained was Themas Skelhorn, who vas employed by Mr. Thomas Abel, Top Locks, Runcorn. Witness asked him what he w tieing with the carriage door open' but he was half asleep and said that he had not opened it. The train then proceeded to Warrington, and witness then got the address of Skelhern. He subsequently heard that a man had been found on the line. Evidence was then given by David Bolton, 'signal man and Thomas Pennington, fireman of a goods train which was passinc the box at the same time that the express was passing. They stated that they saw deceased stand- ing with the door open. Belton further added that'he saw the deceased step from the carriage platforrrs as coolly as if he were alighting from a, carriage in a station.

At this point a telegram was received from the man Skelhorn, stating that he was engaged as a witness at the Liverpool Assizes and was unable to appear at the inquest. The Coroner then read the following signed statement from Skelhorn :—He (Skelhorn) lived at 118, Knutsford- road, Warrington. He entered a third-class compart- ment at Liverpool on Friday afternoon. Just as the train was moving off another man got in the carriage. He was smoking a pipe, and witness told him that it was not a smoking compartment. The man then put the pipe in his pocket and said he could do without a smoke until he got to Warrington. Skelhorn then fell asleep, and was awakened at Sankey by the guard who had found the door open. When the train proceeded on its journey he tried to remember what had become of his fellow-traveller. He then conjectured that he, must have jumped from the carriage, and at Warrington he gave the gu'ard his name and address. Whenehe spake to deceased about smoking be noticed that he had a very strange appearance. The Coroner remarked that the statement was a very straightforward one, and he saw no reason why the in, quest should be adjourned. .• Dr. o:Neil stated that he had examined the body, and was of Opinion that death was due to concussion of the brain caused by the injury received in the fall on the metals. An open verdict was accordingly returned. •

TllESDAY.—Before,his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes.

A DISPUTED Bzr,L.—George IWye-rs, irocer, Weston Point, sued Richard Stevenson, of, Weston Point for £l4 17s. 61. for goods supplied. *r. P. A. Lake rdefended. 'nap defence was that defendant had not given his wife any authority to pledge his credit while at thO same time his wife denied contracting the debt. Defendant bad paid in instalments an account- of about £2, and when he had finished paying 'that account another amount of £l4 was sprung on him as a separate debt, contracted by his wife ithout his knowledge.. TIIE PARAFFIN LAMP OUTRAGE; AT . WARRINGTON.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on .Thursday before Alderman Maudley, • Jolla. Hares was again brought up on a charge of, ir.flicting grievous bodily harm to Ann H'‘res, his wife, by throwing a lighted paraffin lamp at•her on the 4th August. • The Chief Contable stated that the prisoner was before the Bonen eight daylkago,and had been remanded until that morning. • Tee Chief Constable' then recapitulated the statement of facts which he gave last week, ad already published. Briefly stated, the prisoner in a drunken passion, seized a lighted paraffin lamp from the mantelpiece, and 'struck his wife with it, 'Setting • her clothing on fire and burning her severely, The woman, the Chief Constable' st4ited, had been in the Infirmary ever since. At 0140: time life as despaired of, and tbe prisoner, was re- drawled to the •cells at Warrington in order that the woman'e depositions might be taken if it became neces- sary. The woman, he was glad to 84y—and the prisoner 'would no donht be.glad to hear—wa-s out of danger, but the doctor at the Infirmary thought it would be at least a -fortnight before she would be able to leave the institu-' tiim,ana come to the Court. He' therefore asked for a longer period of remand. The prisoner could now be sent ter Straogeways Gaol as his presence here was no longer 110ees5ary.,--The prisoner was remanded to Strartgeways•Gaol until next Friday,

LOCAL TRlPS.—Blackpool was the place selected by the nOthorittes for the annual visit'of the Lymm United Non- conformistSunclay Schools.and early on Monday morning two trains left Lymrn for•this favourite watering place. The day was fortunately favourable*. and all seemed to benefit by the few hours at the seasir &Wars of the Lymm Congregational Sunday Schools had their annual trip on Rushbearing 'Monday, when they Visited Overton Hills. The party, numbering about 50, left Lymtn in brakes early in the morning,, the drive along the country lanes to Frodsham being thoroughly enjoyed., All had 'dinner and tea together, the youngsters; afterwards indulged in games. The party were in charge of Mr. and Mrs. Astley„ Miss Moston and giss Nightingale. . • „ THE HIISIIBEARINC}.—For many years the celebrations connected with the annual festival have dwindled away. The former ceremony of decorating the rush cart and marching in procession to the Parish Church to hold ser- vice and strew the aisles with new rushes his been , abandoned. Three troops of naorris dancers paraded the, village on Saturday, but the dancers seemed to lack A‘ go " compared with the wild caperings of former years. Since the wakes, part of the festival was removed from the .neighbourhood of the Cross, and held in a field in Pypp,,r-street, and there the swings, shooting galler- ies, &1., were congregated this year, to be patronized as usual by the younger members of the population.

MOST READERS

Rave had some experience of sick rooms, either at patients or attendants. How many such are there who have not felt the weakness or debility which follows the eickness, more discouraging than even the weakness it- self? Though the disease may have given way before the timely application of proper remedies, and though.in consequence, no immediate danger is to be-ipprehended, the slow progress towards convalescence is very dis- heartening to patient and attendant alike. Hew often do we hear people say :—" Yes. The fever has left him. He is free from any immediate danger. Itut ha is so weak. If only he coeld regain strength ! "

Tbisdebilitating effect of fever and other diseases is indeed a great source of anxiety to medical men as well as to their patients ; and anything which would tend .directly to shorten the !period between the departure of the fever and the complete recovery of strength would be naturally welcomed by both doctors and patients.

This is one of the reasoßs why Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters is so popular, why doctors prescribe it so largely, why chemists sell it so generally, why patients have re- course to it SO readily, and why everybody has a good word to say for it. Not only does it attack the disease in its stronghold, not only does it drive the evil from the system, but it gives back directly the strength of which the system has been deprived by illness; it infuses new life into the blood, and the recovery of health and strength, instead of being, as it otherwise would be, dis- couragingly slow, intermittent, and uncertain, becomes, when this excellent medicine is resorted to,encouragingly rapid, regular, and certain. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A LOCAL CYCLE-RACER WITFIOITT LAMP.

On Monday morning at the Warrington Borough Police Court before the Mayor, Mr. F. Monks, and Alder- man Harrison, Thomas Astill, the well-known bicycle racer, was charged with having on the 4th August ridden his bicycle at 11.15 p.m.without light.—Sergeant Hollingworth deposed that on the night in question he 3aw defendant riding up Winwick-street without a lighted lamp. He went to him and Astill dismounted and said he had only cr.me from the station having just returned from Rainford. He was one of the most famous riders in this part of the country and had experience anough to know better. He had nit far to go home.— Astill stated that he bad walked part of the way and then got on his machine as the street was quiet. He was only riding about 6 miles an hour.—The Bench ordered him to pay 53.

A. CONSTABLE AND CO.—From these publishers we have received a pretty little story entitled " Torriba (a princess of the Amorayes)," which is from the pen of J. Cameron Grant. The narrative is founded on the chance acquaintance which a young Englishman makes with Torriba, whose home is in Brazil. Ile finds her a wild,untutored child of nature, but her beauty, ionocence and simplicity at once attract him- Time rolls on ana a mutual affec- tion springs up between the two, but marriage and " happiness ever after" was not for them, a great calamity severing their hopes decisively. Life in the Brazilian forests as experienced by these two, the author portrays vividly, and in fact draws a picture of an idyllic existence. There is abundance of pathos in the story, which is altogether simply yet cleverly woven. None can fail to be delighted with the heroine's charming personality. A freedom of treatment,somawhat unusual, is not infrequently displayed, and it further redounds to the author's credit as a departure from cenventionality. A strong cloth cover neatly embellished, encloses the pages of the story and also a useful appendix at the end.—" The Enemies," by E. Hs Cooper, will be found to be a more than ordinarily interesting novel. Briefly it tells of love and marriage,temptation and almost a fall, finishing up with reconciliation and peace. This is quite the orthodox and well-worn foundation of a novel,and it might be thought that little of interest could be woven into any new a- doption of it. "The Enemies," however, is original, and embodies characters and incidents of an admirable order. The political and social world finds a large place in the tale, to which it lends variety. Nothing sensa- tional enters into it, and the pleasure which its perusal affords further proves that the old style of story—an intricate plot with characters of every hue--is not necessary to claim the attention of modern readers. Coming down to minor details we musl- in justice say that the binding of the story is Most COMM,3ll,4l,bie, so artistic and pretty is the simple design of the cover. Readable type and rich paper complete the excellence of the edi- tion.—There are over 200 pages of very interesting reading in "Sketches and Stories, Grave and Gay," by Montgomery Carmichael. Most of the items in the book have already appeared in some of the leading English Magazines, but they can be no worse for that, rather better if anything, for their publication in those journals is but a guarantee of their excellence. It is also very unlikely that more than one or two have been read by the same person 80 they will rank as new and quite original to the mejosity of readers. Of widely diversified charac- ter, the sketches, etc., form a budget of literature which will suit all. Like other volumes of the same class it may be picked up, put down, and resumed at any time, with its attractiveness still fresh and strong. Messrs. Constable and Co. have issued the volume with characteristic taste and get up.—Still another novel published by the same firm remains to be commented upon. A. lady writer, Mrs. Newman, is the author, and" His Vindication," as a standard of her work, should rank high in the public estima- tion. " Too late," and " Jean," by the same author have already attracted favourable notice and this last venture should hold its own anywhere. Mrs. Newman commences her story by introducing us to the gossips of Greyhorough, the town round which the characters of " His Vindication " move and have their being- These three gossips, each different and each cleverly conceived, provide a bye-play to the more important events of the story. Their idiosyncracies are amusing in the extreme. Early in the narrative the guardian of Barbara Vane, the heroine, dies, and her fortune is found to have disappeared, presum- ably by his wrongful speculation. Ruin follows the death and the family falls from its high estate. An interesting account of their after life occupies the middle of the book, in which Barbara Pane is misunderstood all round and her actions wrong- fully interpreted. By her, however, the ultimate vindication of her guardian comes about and with it the happiness of all concerned. There is a villian in the story and numerous other characters, all truly excellent. That!of Miss Denison, a lady who makes Barbara the instrument whereby all is cleared up, is worthy of study. She is both cynical and loving and possesses it keen insight into the char- acter of others. Mrs. Newman'is whole construction of the tory is artistic and masterly. Nothing is over-done, vet, nothing is commonplace and it is evident that care has bben taken in the perfecting of all details of the story so that everything in it is coherent and consistent. " His Vindication " will be well liked wherever it, goes. -

A PECULIAR DISEASE.

The fretting and wearing cares of business, domestic relations, political life and other affairs, with laborious brain-work, social dissipation, and unhygienic habits, have caused such an increase in the number of invalids from nervous exhaustion, that this disease is now re- coen.3ed as a distinct malady. Bodily health and welfare are neglected for wealth, position, honour and distinction. The business man, by close application to his business, hurried and irregular meals, loss of sleep and the neglect of exercise, at last finds his health failing and himself incapable of meeting the demands on his energies. So it is with a great many people. They eat in a hurry, sleep as if in a hurry and very little, toil long and late, with little or no recreation, until failing health warns them of this sad neglect of duty. This tendency, which is becoming general, has for its outcome but one result—impairment of the phy- sical and incapacity of the mental energies. Ask a physician why it is, and he tells us the blood has become impure from the ccumulation of effete matters and waste materials, which the kidneys have failed to carry off. But why, we ask, have the kidneys not performed their excreting functions? Because the energies and forces of the system have been attracted in another direction by, this intense application to business, and the pursuit of wealth,which has become such a lotttle. Again, we ask, is there any remedy to keep these organs in a healthy condition, or any method by which poisonous products like uric acid, can be eliminated from the circulation ?

The answer is as decided as it is satisfactory. For a Tong time, tests and experiments were made with vari- ous remidies, but to no purpose. At last was found the remedy par excellence above others, Warner', Safe Cure. This great remedy acts as a true specific for this disease, and has been repeatedly and satisfactorily tested. It not only cleanses the blood of all impurities and poison- ous materials, but promotes th 4 healthy activity of the various organs of digestion, circulation and secretion. That this remedy does produce these desirable results,is attested by its over-increasine popularity.

KHAMA'S WEDDING.

An eye-witness thus describes the recent wedding of Khama, the Bamangwato chief :—The old proverb " Happy is the bride that the sun shines on "was cer- tainly a herald of happiness to Khama and Sethagwane, for though in a country like this fair weather is more or less of a certainty, still on this occasion the sun seemed to shine, if possible, with more brilliancy, and yet lack- ing its terrible power of scorching. At an early hour all the village was up and stirring. Streams of smoke curl- ing from hundreds of huts showed busy preparations for an early breakfast, for on this day Khama, chief of the great Bamangwato nation, was to be married to Sating- wane. No daughter of royal blond, no nobly born mai 'en was she, hut a simple village beauty, whose father was chief herdsman to Kham _ _

Thera is much interest attached to this wedding for various reasons. Khama having been a widower for so long, and finding life lonely, now all his children had arown up and were married, and none left to look after him and attend to his needs, wisely resolved to take another wife.

He first paid his deysirs to the daughter of a neigh- bouring chief, hut broke off the eneagement as the rela- tives of the bride required the usual payment ; but Khams, who is a Christian, and follows the observances of Christianity, refused to buy his wife at all, and so was compelled te look elsewhere. While returning from his unsuccessful quest his eyes tell an the young maiden as she followed her father carrying his food to the dis- tant cattle post, and he felt that he here had found the the one to make him happy and tend him in his old age. Besides, wasn't she a Christian too? And hadn't he seen her at church on Sundays? All preliminaries with the parents were settled and the wedding-day fixed. But what makes this marriage of such importance is the fact of its bein* the first marriage of its kind ever celebrated in this tribe according to the rites of the Church. Nine a.m. Was the hour fixed for the ceremony. Long before that hour crowds of natives could be seen assembling on the outskirts of their kraals. The veneration and res- pect with which Khama is held, not only by his own na- tion hut more especially by the white population, was fully shown by the vast crowds who wended their way to the little mission church, snugly hidden away an.ense the trees on the hillside. It was a day of holiday. Even the stores were closed in honour of the event, and every man considered it to be his duty to do honour to the chief by being present at his wedding. Khama and his best man were the first te arrive. The bridegroom Was dressed in white nankee trousers, an exceedingly well-made frock-coat, white silk tie, turned•down collar, white shirt, and carried a pair of lavender gloves in his hands. Meceleksreste was the name of the gentleman who waste help Kha,ma,and hold his hat, ,te.. At length a deep, humming noise from the throats of the many hundreds otesicie the church proclaimed the arrival of the bride, who was escorted up the aisle by one of the principal white storekeepers, and took her seat alongside her fiance on two of four chairs which were placed with their sacks to the rest of the congregation and near to the table on whieh lay the register and press and ink. She wore a short walking dress of white satin, most plainly made, puff sleeve' s a long white veil over her head, and a large bunch of artificial orange blossoms on it, which was kept occa- sionally in place by a broad band of elastic round the bask of her head ; unfortunately the elastic was a trifle loose, and occasionally the bunch hung over the side of an ear, but the bride evidently experienced little or no inconvenience, and the defect was soon remedied. Black boots and stockings completed her attire. The service was performed by Mr. Willoughby, the Wesleyan missionary, and was entirely in the Sechwane language, and parrs ef it were most impressive through the earnest way in which the whole congregation joined in the singing. The usual fermula of asking if any im- pediment was known was gone through, this being one of the parts of the ceremony insisted An by the Govern- ment. the service being under the old Du,ch and Roman law, as used through all the Protectorate. The bride- gr OM and bride then joined hands, end the farmer made the following dee' tration :—" I, Khama, call all the assembly here to witness and to testify that I take you. Sethagwane, to be my wife, and to build our house together until we die," the bride repeating the same words, with the exception of saying :—" I, Sethaerwane, sin takers by Khama," Sse. Then comes the declaration that they are man and wife. A lectere on love and its consequent duties is given by Mr. Willoughby, and a prayer and the Benedictus brings to a close the simple and impressive ceremnny. When all was over the bride and bridegroom adjourned to Mr. Willeughby's house, where a reception was held, and cake awl tea given to all tense who cared for it. The ceremony of cutting the cake was duly performed by the bride, who seemed much to appreciate the eating of

NOTES AND NEWS.

A strange scene took place at a wedding ceremony at Bedford. The bride had made the expresso stipulation that the wedding was to be a perfectly quiet one, but somehow news of the forthcoming event leaked out,with the result that a large congregation assembled, and the bridal pair found themselves the centre of an admiring throng. The bridegroom got all the blame for this un- welcome publicity, and in vain did he plead that the ceremony should proceed. The bride protested that she could not stand the ordeal of going through the ser- vice before so many eves,and was deaf to all entreaties. The registrar exhan tel his patience, and hastened oat of the building, followed by the greons--elect and the principal witnesses. Then the minister in attendance' had the pleasure of escorting the timid bride to a cab, and she was conveyed back to the parental roof-tree.

The " Atlanta Constitution," which boasts the presence of Uncle Remo:, and the Georgia poet, Frank L. Stanton. on its staff, is responsible for the following story, the troth of which it vouches for :—" A good- looking, well-to-do bachelor, of Griffin, Georgia, was being teased by the young ladies of a club for not being married. He said. " marry the one of you whom, on a secret vote, you Pint to he my wife." There were nine members of the club. Each 241 went into a corner and used great cauticn in preparing her ballot and die- guising her handwriting. The result of the vote was that there were nine votes cast, each girl receiving one. The man remains a bachelor, the club is broken up, and the girls are mortal enemies, united in the one de- termination that they will not speak to the man again.

"Give me a drink before you bury me." Such (says the Paris c rrespondent of the "Daily Telegraph") were the words gravely uttered by a presumed corpse as it was about to be laid in the coffin. The bystanders, ap- palled by this phenomenon, took to ffi,ht, and reported the extraordinary incident to their chief, who hurried t the spot to find the suppo4ed dead man had by that time been completely revived by a physician, and was on his way to the hospital, where no doubt as to his per- fect recovery is now entertained. An Arab named Mo- hammed Ben Aed el Benzerti had murdered no less than four of his fellow-countrymen, and for this crime he had been condemned to the capital penalty. The execution came off on Saturday morning, and, according to the regulations, the murderer had to remain suspended for a quarter of an hour ere the rope was cut; but while those ,s7ho were charged to carry out the sentence maintain that this rule was obeyed to the letter, the police who were on the spot declare that Mohammed only spent seven minutes dangling in mil-air. Be this as it may, the criminal passed through a rude ordeal, and emerged therefrom, if not kicking, at least with some breath left in his body. The opinion in Tunis is that the death sen- tence will now be commuted.

According to the " Matabele Times," Rhodesia has had a narrow escape. The story runs that after the Matabeleland Mounted Police left for Pitsani, there were not ten mounted police left in the whole of Matabele- land. It was only owing to a hitch somewhere that the whole of the Rhodesia Horse did not march to the bor- der as well. "Thank God "(proceeds OUT contempor- ary) " for that hitch ! For had they gone, and taken all the remaining field guns and Maxims, as intended, the Matabele would have d.ne with the whole of Rhode- sia what they are stated to have done with the township of scoffed' it. Every white man,woman, and child woul(i have traen wiped out completely, and Bulawayo would have been razed to the ground.

Any doubt cast upon the wholesomeness of the rabbit as an article of food will alarm a very large proportion of the humbler p-mulation of this country. Thanks to the reproductive habits of the rabbit itself, and also to the fact that it serves as an object of sport to so many People. its flesh forms one of the cheapes- articles of animal fond. Dr. S'evenson, however, the eminent analyst to the Home Office, stated in his evidence at the inquest on the servant who died of eating rabbit-pie,that in the course of his experience he had found large num- bers of live rabbits affected with micrc,-organisms to such an alarminc, extent that they frequently died in consequence. The organisms. he added, were generally killed by cooking, but this could not be taken as a safe- guard. The danger, it would seem, csnnot be very great; otherwige, from the enormous number of rabbits eat(--n, we should hear of more cases of It is a curious fact that meat cooked in a pie often seems to develop some poisonous qualities. The number of people who have been made ill from eating beefsteak-pie, for instance, is surprising

The training of does for military Purposes has long been cultivated in the Jager regiments (Chasseurs a pied) of the German Army. In the so-called Jaga.r Battalion of the Guard at Potsdam no leos than thirty dogs of various kinds are employed for this purpose. The care of the animals, which must be vary young, is entrusted to reliahle people - but a change in their atteniants is frequently effected an that the do.a'may be accustomed more to the unifnrm than to the individual. The train- ing begins by causing the animals to accompany single patrols on sentinels' walks, the dogs being first led by a string, but later permitted to run free. Having ac- customed them to the uniform, the next step is to nen them hack, and they soon understand that they are en- trusted with messages. written on a slip of paper which is exactly so large that it can he concealed behind the copper plate en the collar. When the patrol examines the latter the animals can scarcely be held back, and as soon as pnssible race impetuously to the main body. It is somewhat more difficult when the dot's have to again find the patrol, who has meantime continued his march, hut the animals qualified for the performance of war, succeed in this also before long. Then the training pro- ceeds step by step. After having learned to take mes- sages, they are taught to search for the wounded, to bring ammunition to companies engaged in battle, and, what is more diffie-alt, to fled the enemy without mak- in. any neise. Of the animals submitted to the batta- lion for training, only a few are capable of satisfying the demands made of a real war dog. As yet -German pointers have s:anwn themselves the best for this pur- pose. English breeds possess a higher intelligence, but go to work too hastily, and consequently tiro more quickly.

FOOTBALL

THE PROPOSED NEW COUNTY CLUB.

4 meeting of the Lancashire section of the Northern Rugby Union was held on Monday night at the Spread Raffle Hotel', Manchester, Mr. J. H. Smith (Widnes) pre- siding. The business first brought forward was the con- sideration of the bvelaws for the new county organisa- tion. The byelaws,which were adopted as recommended, included the following :—" 1. That the name of the union be '-The Lancashire County Football Union,' and that its headquarters be in Manchester, where all general meetines behold. 2. The Union shall he the paramount authority on the conduct of the game in accordance with the regulations of the Northern Rugby Football Union throughout Lancashire, and shall be governed by a committee of 16 members. 3. The following clubs, as founders of the union ,viz., lirrmehton Rangers, Legh, Oldham, Rechdale Hornets, St. Helens, Tyldeslev. War- rington, Whines, and Wigan, shall each be entitled to elect one member of the committee. The remaining seven seats shall be filled at the annual general meeting of the union, to be held in July in each year. 6. The committee shall elect the county teams and fill up vacan- cies on the committee, and may exercise all the powers of the club."—Mr. J. E. Platt, the hon. secretary. re- nerted that a difficulty had arisen with regard to the filling un of the fixture lists ef some of the junior (+lbs. He suggested that 'to meet the difficulty two junior leasues should be formed, and for that reason the county divided into two narts. The meeting resolved that a meeting of the clubs in the union'outside the senior com- petition be called with a view of forming a junior league or leagues, and that such junior clubs as were desirous of joining the league or leagues be invited to do so.— Mr. Platt suggested the following as possible lige for the two suggested junior leagues, with the addition of such clubs as might be disposed to join:—(1) Salford St. Bartholomew's, lironehton Recreation, Stockpert ibn- gers, Abbey Hills, Lees, Oldham Juniors, Waterhead Hornets, Cromnten " A," St. Clement's " A," and Duk- infield " A," (2) Runcorn Victoria, Rencern Recreation, Warrington St. Mary's, Warrington Locomotive. Latch- ford Rangers, Tvldeslev Recreation, Atherton Hornets. Swinton Cherch. and St. Helens Recs. "A."—There was no other business.

SHIP CANAL.

LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

- During the week ended August 7th 146 vessels,with an aggregate net registered tonnage of 43,724 tons, entered and cleared from the port of Manchester, against 185 veasels'of 54,291. tons in the precedinir week. Of these 107 vessels of 18,525 tens entered from or cleared for home ports. foreign trade being represented by 39 ves- sels of 25,199 tons. The foreign arrivals included four steamers and three sailing ships with timber 'from the Baltic and North America,five steamers with flints from France,three steamers with cotton,grain,fruit, and other produce from the Mediterranean,a steamer with ore from Spain, and a barque with logwood from Yucatan. Steamers sailed with general cargoes to Copenhagen, Stettin. Gothenburg, and Bombay ,and vessels cleared for West Bay, Savannah, Wilmintton. Mirainishi, Ship Island, St. Thomas (Canada), Frederikstadt and Memel. There were the usual numerous arrivals and departures in connection with the regular weekly set-- vices. Ninety-six head of cattle and eight horses were tanded on Friday by the Dublin steamer.

WARRINGTON ASSESSMENT COM- MITTEE,

A meeting of this Committee was held in the Board Room at the Workhouse. on Friday. Mr. G. Pilling oc- cupied the chair, and there were also present Messrs. A. J. Jolley, A. Jolley, W. Houghton, J.P., J. Biltinge, J. Warburton, J. Clarke, John Houghton, with Mr, Sutton (clerk), Mr. Owen (valuer), and Mr. Tunstall (assistant overseer.) A number of appeals of a minor character, dealing chiefly with house property, were considered, and in many cases the assessments were reduced. The valuer (Mr. Owen) in his report stated that "The engine house for the Pearsen Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., is not yet completed in the Bewsey works. The reservoir is for condensing purposes only and will not be in use until the new engine is completed. At the present time there is no beneficial occupation."—" The Firth Company are in occupation of Friars Green Mill, and of Florence Mill 4 into the last of which they are gradually removing their plant from Friars Green Mill so as to vacate it entirely in the ,first quarter of next year. The assessments appealed against at the last meeting are as under Florence Mill gross £370, rateable £3OO ; Friars Green gross £7 53., rateable £6 ; Friars Green gross £162 .53., rateable £13.5. I have inspected both works and find' the transition of plant from one to the other is proceeding gradually. The assessment on Florence Mill is that which was paid when it was a cotton mill in full opera- tion. I think it would be a fair settlement if the Friars Green assessments stand as they are and a temporary assessment be put on Florence Mill for its present oc- cupation 'of gross £2OO rateable £l6O. When Florence Mill is wholly occupied by the company a new valuation will be required tor assessment."—With reference to the National Telephone Company, Limited,the valuer stated that inasmuch as from the 4th April last the Postmaster General tcok possession uf 262 miles of wires out of 342 miles in the seven townships in the Union and they hav- ing consented to a reduction of the assessments in this respect he had reduced the assessment to £79 53. gross £67 15i.—With regard to Messrs. Richard Evans and Company's new siding from the Liverpool, St. Helens, and South Lincashire Railway he assessed it at £46 103. gross,aud £42 103. rateable. He had written to the St. Helens overseers asking them whether they had com- menced to rate the railway but had received no reply.— The rep )rt was adopted.

ALLEGED BABY FARMING AT NEW

FERRY.

At New Ferry Police Station, near Birkenhead,Richai-d Joseph Brindley, groom and gardener at Bromborough Vicarage, and his wife, Louisa Brindley, wet() charged in custody with having abandoned a male child at Liver- pool on July I.—lnFpector-Detective Pearson, of Chester, visited the prisoner's cottage at New Ferry. on Saturday, and made enquiries respecting a child whom they had charge of whilst residing at Penkridge, Staffordshire. The woman made a statement to the effect that they left Penkridge on July 1 with their belongings, and pro- ceeded to Liverpool where, being tired, she gave the child to a girl to hold %Vbile she looked round the shops. On returning the girl and child had disappeared, and nothing had been heard of the latter since. The husband, when questioned, denied all that his wife had said and stated that the child was a long distance away, but could be produced ; it bad been given in charge of a woman to nurse. Several weeks before the prisoners left Penkridge the police found in their cottage several letters relating to children. In one ease it was stated the prisoners agreed to adopt a child for £6. There was also a large box of children's clothing and linen of superior quality, a sealskin sacque coat, a valuable opera cloak, and three feeding. bottles. The prisoners were remandeil for a week. e police are endeavouring to trace the children referred to in the letters found at the prisoner's house, but in many cases the addresses have been torn off.

SOUTEI WARRINGTON V. TIMPRRLEY.—The runners-up in the first annual contest by second class county teams met at Crofts Bank on Saturday to play off for the lead- ing award. The game was spiritedly competed, and much skill displayed in delivery. Some of the Warring- ton wins were of a heavy character, in five instances by fully half of the total score and in the final round " 21 —love." Timperley secured but three successes, but were within the old point of a fourth. The competition was instituted in May last,with the view of encouraging Promising players to render themselves eligible for first- class teams, and sixteen districts entered. Mr. H. Jarvis, Withington Midland, was referee. Score : WLIIRAMiIeit. TIM P RALEY. J Watkins__ 2l 7 Whitelegge 8 I Webb I Bird 2l. A. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN. The death of Mr. AMPS; Smith, a member of the District council. removes from the Council a very useful member, and one who will be very much missed by his coney/nes. for his business qualities. Fre was associated with Mr. Borron and Mr. Beatty for the Crow Lane Ward, and although he was opposed at the last election in March by a strong local candidate he was successful in being returned by a large majority. Mr. Smith was a man of great .xpprience In township work. having been a Mayor of Wigan, and up to his death was an alderman Of that borough. On many occasions his advice has been found of much value in the discussions in the Council.

Arrangements are now in progress for the elec- fion of a member to fill the vacancy and it is to be hoped that, the ratepayers will see to it that no member is retnrwd who will, for instance,counten- ance such reactionsry—not to say silly—resolutions as that which Mr. Gilmour succeeded in placing on he Gencral Purposes Committee's minutes. It is for the interests of the ratepayers that the fullest nuhlicity shnivld be given to the prnceedings of a nubile body like the District Council. Yet by the passing of this resolution the ratepayers are pre- vented from obtaining any preliminary knowledge of the Council's proceedings. The business will he -at and dried in committee nod will be placed be- fore the Council and passed before the public have my opportunity of seeing. what is being done. Most other public bodies are advancing in the di- rection of fuller publicity Newton District Coun- cil takes a step backwards. In Warrington all the important business is printed'in the local news- Papers the week before the Town Council meeting. In St. Helens. reporters actually attend and give Full reports of tile meetings of some of the most ivnportant corntnittees'of the Corporation. Yet the .atepayers of Parlestown and Newton are not to have even a glimpse at their own business until it is finally settled and disposed of.

The District Council have at, last received the new steam roller, and, better still, have lost no time in getting it at work. The roads have, during the oast two or three months got into a very loose state Ind the presence of a steam roller was very muoh needed for the purpose of crushing refractory stones into their DiflCPq. I suppose now that there is no excuse, we shall have our roads kept in excellent repair.

ef, •

Haydock has been most unfortunate this week in losing two of its benefactors by the hand of death, viz., Mr. Arthur Evans and Miss Ruth Evans. Mr. Evans has for a long period been a resident of Hay- dock but lately had become more a bird of passage, and the news of his death at Bayreuth in Bavaria has come as a great surprise to the inhabitants. Ile was a great friend to Haydock, and was always to the fore in all matters which were for the good of his fellow men. Miss Evans was also a generous benefactor, and there is no doubt that she Will be greatly missed.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Council wag held in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Monday. Mr. Borron, J.P., occupied the chair, and there were also present the Rev. E. W. W. Kiye, Messrs. -Collingwood, Emmett, J.P., McMahon, Gilmour, Wood, Simpson. Beatty, Thompson, and Worsley (law clerk), Shirley (clerk), and Brierley (surveyor).

THE -EXAMINER! SATURDAY AUGUST 15,. 1396

the roof off the building to meet the requirements of thn Council, and he did not see why the solicitor should hare gone further in the matter.—Mr. Gilmour said it was unbusinesslike to withdraw the summons without the consent of the Council.—The Chairman said if Mr. Gil- mour had gone carefully into the matter as their 8011ci. tor and surveyor had done, he might have changed his stron' expressions and views. Mr. Ronayne had built entirely on his own land, and by taking the roof off he had altered his construction from a building to a post- ing station. He considered it would have been per- secution to proceed any further with the action, and to get the man into prison again by prosecuting him for £4O which was the amount of the fine Mr. Ronayne had incurred. He did int think any of the electors would have endorsed such a proceeding as sug- gested.—Mr. Gilmour : I never suggested it.—The Chairman : As I have gathered, it has been suggested in this room —Mr. Gilmour said he would be the last man to send Mr. Ronayne to prison, but he must have an explanation.—The Chairman : You cannot ask any- thing further. There is no other busines3. The Law Clerk had replied that the matter was put in his hands. He had dealt with it in a legal an practical manner. If he had taken any other course, the dignity of the Council would have been very much impaired.—Mr. Gilmour said he was still in the dark as to why the sum- mons was withdrawn. He wanted the public to know. The Chairman and solicitor had bungled over the matter. —Mr. Emmett asked whether the building was on Mr. Ronayne's land entirely.—The Chairman said he had already stated that whatever the man had erected was on his own land.—Several other questions were asked, hut Mr. Horror' vacated the chair and several members left the room, and the meeting broke up.

NEWTON-LE-WILLOWS AND DISTR TOT HORTICULTURAL SHOW AND SPORTS.

The eleventh annual show of fruit, flowers, and vege- tables, and the second annual athletic sports held in coa- nection with the Newton-le-Willows and district Horti- cultural Society, were held on Saturday on the ground of the Eirlestown Football Club. The day was of a most wretched description, arid the attendance was in consequence of a meagre character. It is a peculiar fact, that on the eleven occasions on which the show has been held, the rain has interfered with its success. The show of fhwerg, fruit, fell far short of previous years, both in quantity and quality, the decrease this year be- ing in some measure due no doubt' to the death of Mr. James Smith of Brookside, who previously was a !tree exhibitor of products. The entries numbered nearly 600, being something like 200,Iess than last year. The judges were Messrs. Brunton, °Amara Hall, Northwich; James Flea.tpa, The Lawn, Aigburth ; Grant Clarke, Statham Ledge, Lynim ; and the judges of the gardens were Messrs. Jackson, Holmes and Schofield. A VOTE OF CONDOLENCE.

The Chairman said it was in no formal manner that he rose before thegeneral business of the Council com- menced to express feelings of deep sorrow and regret that since the last meeting a valued member of the Council had paosed away in death. A few months ago the late Mr. James Smith was again elected to his seat on that Council, but never was able to resume those labours which he so ungrudgingly gave in the discharge of his public duties—duties which he performed with ability, energy, ?nd straightforwardness, commanding the respect of the Council, officials and general public. While deploring their loss and the great loss to the dis- trict, they could sympathise with the widow and family in their bereavement, and he desired to move a vote of condolence with them, and that the same be recorded on the minutes of that Council. He, therefore, moyod " Chat this Council has heard with deep sorrow and re- tret of the death of Mr. James Smith, -Pet- Brookside. Newton-le•Willows, one of the past memhers saf this Council, Chairman of the Gas and Water,Committee, 'Alderman of the Borough of Wigan, and4rnistice of the Peace,and that this Council•desires to express its appre- ciation and recognition of the valuable services rendered by Mr. Smith to this Council and district, and 1 p place on record its deep regret at the great loss this f-Couneil and district have sustainA by his death and that a copy of this resolution be passed under the common seal of the Council and be forwarded to Mrs. Smith as a res- pectful expression to her and to her sorrowing family of the sympathy and condolence of this Council in their deep sorrow and afilhetion."—Mr. CrilMOLlr, cogeltd the proposition, and stated that he had not the slightest doubt that Mr. Smith in all his work was animated with a desire for the good of the town and district. GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE.

The Chairman moved the adoption of this committe's minutes. Referring to the resolution that a printed copy of the committee's minutes—one for each reporter —be placed on the reporter's table at the Council meet- ings,Mr. Gilmour, who seconded the committee's recom- mendations, said that he did not propose the resolution with the intention of crippling the press, but in order that they might have reports of the whole of the pro- ceedings as they were confirmed by the Council. He was in favour of as much publicity of the Council's proceed- ings as possible. hut someone had taken an undue:ad- vantage of publishing the minutes before the Council meeting. He thought they should not be given before being confirmed by the Council, and his resolution had that obPct in view.--The minutes were then passsd.

TES MEDICAL OFTICEE OP lIRALTH.

In accordance with notice given the Chairman moved the reappointment of Dr. J. W. Watkins as medical officer of health for the Urban district of Newton-in- Makerfidd for one year at a salary of £so.—Mr. Thomp- son seconded, and the resolution was unanimously carried.

THE VACANCY IN CROW LAN WARD.

The Chairman said he had received notice that owing to the decease of the late Mr. James Smith there was a, vacancy for a councillor for Crew-lane Ward.—Mr. Lol- ling wood proposed, and Mr. Beatty seconded, and it was agreed to,that the seat be declared vacant, and:the clerk be authorised to fill the vacancy at as early a date as possible. TUE PROSECUTION OF MR. OA.RRATT RONATNR. Mr. Gilmour said he wished to know by whose author- ity the summons against Mr. Ronayne was withdrawn ? He thought it was only right and fair that when the Council took proceedings the press and the Council ought to know what had been done. He therefore wished to ask the Law Clerk by whose authority the Summons was withdrawn.—The Chairman said :Mr. Gilmour had not given notice of such a question. So far as he (the chair- man) remembered, the matter was placed entirely in the hands of the solicitor to do as he thought best.—The Law Clerk reviewed the proceedings which had been taken in the case. Mr. Ronayne !had complied with the re- quirements of the C-aincil in removing the roof from the building he erected, and which answered the object the Council had, in taking Tweeedings. He (Mr. Worseley) therefore withdrew the summons. The course taken by the Council had been entirely vindicated. —Mr. Gilmour asked the Chairman whether he sent a man to assist Mr. Ronayne to take the roof off.— rhe.Chairman said he did not know why he should be asked to tell his own private affairs to the members of the Council, or to answer for anything he did in a private capacity. He therefore declined to answer the question.—Mr. Gilm)ur said he did not be- lieve in having a chairman who acquiesced in the action Which had been taken. He charged the solicitor of the Council with not conducting the proceedings in a proper inanuer. Their solicitor ought to have obtained an in- junction to pull the erection down. As it was their solicitor and chairman had made a "bungle" of it.— The Law Clerk: I think that goes a little too far.—Mr. Emmett said he understood that the erection was partly on Mr. Ronayne's own ground and partly on ground be- longing to the Council. He had seen the building and it did not appear to him that the front of the building had been in any way altered. He noticed that the roof had been sawn off. He had always understood that it wis encroaching on the Council's ground.—The Law Clerk: No.—Mr. McMahon @aid Mr. Ronayne had paid he penalty for int, inging the bylaws of the Council by serving a term of imprisonment. The pnblie. sense ofokie itideshAtallifsid* the diStrit in the derith'Off one w h~Acir lilkaileitOrivilill"6l44"""allilii4 was expressed in a very remarkable manner, and as the cortege wended its way through the town there were general tokens of regret and sympathy manifested. The cortege left the residence of the deceased gentleman at 10 o'clock, and accompanied by the members of the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council, proceeded to St. Peter's Church. The councillors present were Messrs. C.B. F. Borron, J.P., C.C., W. Collingwood, J. W. Emmett, J.P.,F. J. Wood,W.Gilmour.J. Simpson,W. Thompson, T. On:nand, W. Beatty, Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, Mr. R. Brierley (surveyor), and Mr. W. W. Shirley (clerk), were also present. The service at the church was very impressive, being conducted by the Rev. H. Monk (vicar of Newton), and the Rev. Mr. Drury. vicar of St. Cather- ine's' Wigan. There was a large congregation, which includei the Rev. J. Carson (rector of Wargrave), Rev. T. W. Johnstone (curate of St. Peter's), Mr. J. E. Pardey, Drs. J. W. and H. Watkins, Mr. W. Holland Owen, Mr. W. A. Mawdsley, Mr. R. Barton, Mr. R. Stuart, Mr. G. Clarke, and others. At the con- clusion of the service the cortege proceeded by road to Wigan being accompanied through Newton by the mem- bers of the Council. Along this part of the road the deepest, sorrow and greatest respect were manifest. The cemetery was reached about half-past twelve, and the coffin was at once borne to the grave, where the remain- ing rites were impressively conducted amid a tremend- ous gathering of people. The body was enclosed in a metallic shell, the outer case being of panelled oak, handsomely.mounted with brass fittings, and bearing the following inscription :-"James Smith, born April 12th, 1034, died August 4th, 1898." The chief mourners rode in four carriages behind the hearse, in the following order :-First carriage, Mrs. Smith, Mrs. W. Woods, Mr. Arthur and Mr. W. Hindley Smith ; secord carriage, Mrs. Bradley, Miss Josephine Smith, Mr. Douglas Smith, Mr. Sidney Smith, and Miss Daisy Hindley ; third carriage. the Misses Pennington, Mrs. Arthur Smith, and Mrs. W. Hindley Smith; fourth carriage, Mr. John Smith, Mr. W. Woods, GDr. F. E. Bradley, and Mr. George Clarke. Numerous gentlemen followed in order in their own private carriages. 'fire following were amongst a host of friends besides the mourners given above,who sent wreaths-Mr. T. Airey, Mr. and Mrs. G. Airey, Mr. and Mrs. R. It. Barton,Mrs. Brown (Golborne), coachman, gardeners, and maids at Newton, Mr. and Mrs. Geerge Clarke, Rev. E. A. Duty, the Hen. T. W. Legh, M.P., Rev. Herbert Monk, Newton Division Conservative Association, Newton and Earlestown Conservative Club,Mr and the Misses Pardey, Captain and Mrs. Royce, Mr. R. and Mrs. Stuart, Mrs. Henry Smith and family, Mr. and Mrs. F. wood, Dr. Watkins and family.

PENKETH.

OLD WIDOWS TREAT.—On Saturday the Penketn widows were taken on their annual trip, the destination teing Llangollen. About 30 widows were conveyed by 'bus to Warrington where they took train from there. A very pleasant day was spent. Mr. P. Nwnall supervised the arrangements in a satisfactors manner. RETURN OF THE BRIGADE.—The St. Mary's Company C.L.8., returned from their week's training on Saturday. The health of the lads was excellent, and all speak in glowing terms of their complete enjoyment. At their inspection by General Eccles, they performed their drills in a manner that gave great satisfaction.

--- WINDOW AND CO r TAGE GARDENING SOCIETY. The third annual exhibition of the above society was held on Saturday. The exhibition this year was held in the Wesleyan school, the Infants School being toe small to accommodate the exhibits. These:lconsisted of fruit and flowers, vegetables, and cage birds. The specimens in each class were very good and in some cases the competition was very keen. There was a good attendance of ladies and gentlemen at the opening of the meeting. Mn. Garnett, the president, made a short address, and then Prefessor Leo Grindon,of Manchester, was c-illed upon. The Professor who is thoroughly con- inirsavt with botanical subjects, gave a very interestiorr address on plants at home and abroad. A. vote of thank's was proposed by Mr. Newcombe to Mr. Grindon, seconded by Mr. Dignum, and carried. Mr. Adam Bell, af Liverpool, judged the birds, and the flowers were again judged by Mr. Kippa,of Walton Lea. Their hit- ting was a treat to viitness, aed both Thewlis and Con- nor came in for severe treatment. Most of the interest in the match ceased after the Warrington total had been exceeded, and at the close of play the score of the homesters stood at 131 for eight wickets.

The meet important of the matches in the Warringtou Cricket League was played on Saturday, when Sankey Wire Mills met the Cheshire Lines Engineers on their own ground. Both have been very successful in their Leagne engagements, and this match was practically the finish of the League as far as position went,for a win for the Wire Mills would place them at dr+ head of the table in an unapproachable position. By losing they would have allowed the Engineers to draw level. A large number of spectators evinced the interest taken in the match by their presence.

The visitors won the toss and decided to occupy the field. Their opponents sent in Walker and Jones. These two made $ stay of but short duration, however, for Simpson and Warburton dismissed each for one run. Hackett and John Monks made" ducks "in succession and a similar fate later fell to Harrison, Howard, and Forster. The score never "looked up" during the whole afternoon and the innings closed for 21 runs. Warburton and Simpson had fine success with the ball, the former taking 5 wickets for seven runs and the latter 4 for ten.

Practically all hope of winning had deserted the homesters when their visitors went to the pitch. Williams and Bailey were the first partners and in them- selves passed the Engineers' score. Both batted well and Williams compiled the top score of 17 whilebis com- panion put together ten. Other fair scores were Hooper. 10 ; Evans, 14; and Cresskill 12. The good total of 98 was reached before all the Sankeyites had been disposed of.

They are now champions of the League and worthily so. Not having lost a match the team has a fine record. Much of this success is due to the esprit de corps which all its members have displayed, and their general effi- ciency in the field.

Sankey Wesleyans were not as successful in their Loague match with St. Elphin's as their co-villagers. They were at home also, but did not delight the home supporters by their commencement. Clare and Upson went in together, but each came back without scoring. Taylor then hit up 13, and after some more weak scoring Llewellyn made another stand by contributing 17. At the close the Wes- leyans had 45 runs to their credit. St. Elphin's did not mak3 a promising. start, for the first el batsmen only scored 14 runs between them. When the Saints were just beginning to feel " blue "Barnes and Woods joined forces and set the fielders to sorne smart leather-hunting. Before a separation was effected the match was won. Woods contributed 8, and later on Bradshaw assisted Barnes and added 12. Barnes himself defied the bowlers and carried out his bat for 45. A majority ot just 40 runs fell to St. Elphins.

The Brewery eleven, as one of the best junior teams in the district, went to Dalton Bank on Saturday with a fair certainty of victory against the Police. Their ex- pectations were realised for knocking up 107 they dis- missed the Police for 32. The latter figure exactly was reached by T. Allen, and two runs in excess of it were made by E. Allen. J. Caine and J. 'Milner, were also largely instrumental in raising the Brewery score to 107.

Sankey Wire Mills and Cheshire Lines Engineers, second teams, played together on the same afternoon as their seniors and the result was similar. The C.L.E's only made 28 runs while their opponents scored 69. Singularly, Cook for Sankey made the same tetal as the Engineers' team. 'rho latter was best represented by Deeley who commenced the innings with " a baker's dozen." rielsby and Rutter had good averages with the ball.

CYCLING.

Since our last issue th-e-m—o--3t—important event in the cycling world of interest to Warringtonians has been the 50 miles championship of the Liverpool and district centre. The race came off on the Stanley Athletic Grounds,Liverpool. There were two local competitors, T. Astill, and J. Waddington. Over 20 riders started but after the pack broke up there were only about a dozen running at all well. These dwindled down until it was seen that the premier place was between Arndt and W. Birtwistle,of Southport. When about 27 miles had been crwered Astill had the misfortune to be "spilled." He was put on again and rode the race out pluckily but could not regain his former lead. Birt- wistle came first,Astill second (only a lap and a half be- hind), and W. Lowe,Rainhill,third. The time was ahrs. 16min. 51 4.54ec5. The winner was the better paced of the two, DAgleish and H. Brown (both professionals), leading him on a tandem.

Saturday was tho president's day of the Warrington Cycling Club and a fair number of members accom- panied Mr. 'l'. Morris to Cranage. Here, as usual,a very jolly time was spent,in which the president participated. A more extended report will be found in another column.

Tuesday being the day for the Lymm Rushbearing festival the destination of the club run was altered and the members cycled to Lymm. Here all joined in the gaities and had what some of them call " a high old time."

WESTON.

FQIIND DEAD IN BED.—On Saturday, Mr. Worsley held an inquest on the body of Ann Dunnigan, aged 52, wife of Mr. Michael Dunnigan,of Company's Row,Weston.The evidence went to show that deceased was found dead in bed by her husband at dinner time on Friday. fie last saw her alive about 5.30 in the morning when he went to work. Dr. McDougall stated that he examined the body but did not find any marks of violence. He was of opinion that de%th was attributable to failure of the heart's action. A verdict in accordance with the medical testimony wae returned.
